0,2026-11-25,"Even if it feels unintuitive, the price of an option does not depend on
whether you expect the underlying to moon or tank. Under some
reasonable market hypotheses, we can build a fully hedged portfolio by
holding the underlying and shorting the option. Since it is riskless,
by no-arbitrage assumption, it must return the same as the risk-free
interest rate. That allows to put together an equation (the
Black-Scholes equation), and solve for the option price (the
Black-Scholes formula).
A European call gives the right, but not the obligation, to buy an
underlying at a fixed price K ⁣K\!K, the strike, on a fixed date
T ⁣T\!T, the maturity. Its value V ⁣V\!V is the premium you need to pay
for having that optionality, and it depends on time and on the
underlying stock price S ⁣S\!S. At maturity it is the payoff1 1
For
example, pay $5 today for the right to buy one AMZN share for $200 in a
month. If AMZN then trades at $230, you buy at $200 and the option is
worth $30. If it trades at $180, you walk away and the option is worth
nothing. 
V(T,S)=max⁡(S−K,0).
  V(T,S)=\max(S-K,0).
V(T,S)=max(S−K,0).
Stock moves scale with price, so it is common to model the returns
instead of the price. Over a short time the return is a small drift
plus noise. The noise is the sum of many small,
unpredictable2 2
Weak-form efficient market hypothesis: past prices do
not predict future moves. 
sources. Such sum can be modeled as a
Brownian motion W ⁣W\!W,3 3
By the central limit theorem, the sum of
many small independent variables is normal. 
so the return is
dSS=μ dt+σ dW ⁣,
  \frac{dS}{S}=\mu\,dt+\sigma\,dW\!,
SdS​=μdt+σdW,
with drift μ\muμ and volatility σ ⁣\sigma\!σ. For our model, we will
assume constant drift, volatility and interest rate r ⁣r\!r. We will also
assume “perfect” trading, with fractional shares allowed, no fees, no
taxes, no dividends, and no arbitrage.
The trick of Black-Scholes is to build a fully hedged portfolio
combining the option and the underlying in the right proportion. By the
no-arbitrage hypothesis, its returns must be the same as the interest
rate r ⁣r\!r, and that way we can put together an equation.
When the stock moves a little, the option moves by Δ\DeltaΔ times as
much, with
Δ=∂V∂S.
  \Delta=\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}.
Δ=∂S∂V​.
If you short one option and hold Δ\DeltaΔ shares, then the two moves
cancel. This is a delta hedge.
One step. From today (left) to
maturity (right), the stock moves from $100 to $110 or $90, and a call at $100 pays $10 or $0. Half a share
minus the call is worth $45 either way, so it is riskless. Without
interest it is also worth $45 today, so the call costs
$50 − $45 = $5.
Note that Δ\DeltaΔ changes with the stock price and time. The
portfolio is riskless only over an instant, and stays so only if it is
rebalanced continuously.
Use the Taylor expansion of the option value and keep the terms up to
order dtdtdt. Since (dW)2=dt(dW)^2=dt(dW)2=dt, the (dS)2(dS)^2(dS)2 term is one of them,
dV=∂V∂tdt+∂V∂SdS+12∂2V∂S2(dS)2.
  dV=\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}dt+\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}dS
  +\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}(dS)^2.
dV=∂t∂V​dt+∂S∂V​dS+21​∂S2∂2V​(dS)2.
Substituting the stock model gives
dV=(μS∂V∂S+∂V∂t+12σ2S2∂2V∂S2)dt+σS∂V∂S dW.
  dV=\left(\mu S\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}
  +\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2S^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}\right)dt
  +\sigma S\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\,dW.
dV=(μS∂S∂V​+∂t∂V​+21​σ2S2∂S2∂2V​)dt+σS∂S∂V​dW.
Now, we can use the same hedged portfolio Π\PiΠ as before, short one
option and long Δ\DeltaΔ shares, which is worth −V+ΔS ⁣-V+\Delta S\!−V+ΔS.
Holding Δ\DeltaΔ fixed over an instant,
dΠ=−dV+ΔdS,
  d\Pi=-dV+\Delta dS,
dΠ=−dV+ΔdS,
and substituting dSdSdS and dV ⁣dV\!dV gives
dΠ=(−∂V∂t−12σ2S2∂2V∂S2)dt.
  d\Pi=\left(-\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}
  -\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2S^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}\right)dt.
dΠ=(−∂t∂V​−21​σ2S2∂S2∂2V​)dt.
See how noise and drift cancel. No dW ⁣dW\!dW term is left, and
everything else is known at time ttt, so over the next instant the
portfolio change is fixed.4 4
A known return with no randomness is
precisely what riskless means. 
We’ve built a riskless portfolio, that
by the no-arbitrage assumption, it must earn the risk-free rate,
dΠ=rΠ dt.d\Pi=r\Pi\,dt.dΠ=rΠdt. Equating both expressions gives us the Black-Scholes
equation
∂V∂t+rS∂V∂S+12σ2S2∂2V∂S2=rV.
  \frac{\partial V}{\partial t}+rS\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}
  +\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2S^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}=rV.
∂t∂V​+rS∂S∂V​+21​σ2S2∂S2∂2V​=rV.
Note that μ\muμ is gone, so the option price does not depend on the
drift.
I won’t bore you with the technical details,5 5
For the details, see
John C. Hull, Options, Futures, and Other Derivatives, 11th edition,
Pearson, 2021. The Bible of derivatives. 
but the above equation can be
solved by a change of variables to turn it into the heat equation, whose
solution is known. Define
τ=T−t,x=ln⁡S+(r−12σ2)τ,V(t,S)=e−rτw(τ,x),
  \tau=T-t,\quad
  x=\ln S+\left(r-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2\right)\tau,\quad
  V(t,S)=e^{-r\tau}w(\tau,x),
τ=T−t,x=lnS+(r−21​σ2)τ,V(t,S)=e−rτw(τ,x),
where www is the option value at maturity. Substituting, all terms
but two cancel, and we get the heat equation
∂w∂τ=12σ2∂2w∂x2.
  \frac{\partial w}{\partial\tau}=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2\frac{\partial^2w}{\partial x^2}.
∂τ∂w​=21​σ2∂x2∂2w​.
With the payoff as initial condition, the solution is
w(τ,x)=12πσ2τ∫−∞∞max⁡(ey−K,0) exp⁡(−(y−x)22σ2τ)dy.
  w(\tau,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2\tau}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
  \max(e^y-K,0)\,\exp\left(-\frac{(y-x)^2}{2\sigma^2\tau}\right)dy.
w(τ,x)=2πσ2τ​1​∫−∞∞​max(ey−K,0)exp(−2σ2τ(y−x)2​)dy.
Completing the squares gives the Black-Scholes formula
V(t,S)=S N(d1)−Ke−rτN(d2),
  V(t,S)=S\,N(d_1)-Ke^{-r\tau}N(d_2),
V(t,S)=SN(d1​)−Ke−rτN(d2​),
where NNN is the standard normal CDF and
d1=ln⁡(S/K)+(r+12σ2)τστ,d2=d1−στ.
  d_1=\frac{\ln(S/K)+\left(r+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2\right)\tau}{\sigma\sqrt{\tau}},
  \qquad
  d_2=d_1-\sigma\sqrt{\tau}.
d1​=στ​ln(S/K)+(r+21​σ2)τ​,d2​=d1​−στ​.
That’s all, lads, go make Ken Griffin broke.

A major report has surfaced this midweek, stating smartphone OEMs are making drastic changes in preparation for 2027. Companies are reportedly preparing to cut back on shipment estimates, and we can attribute it to only one thing.
Tipster Jukan on X highlighted a report by Jiemian (Chinese), saying smartphone makers are lowering their expected 2027 shipment totals. Major companies such as Samsung are reportedly lowering next year's shipments by almost 10%. That would reduce its totals to ~210 million units. Another eye-catching name is Apple. The publication claims Apple is cutting its phone shipments by 5.6% in 2027, bringing units to around ~233 million.
For Motorola, estimated shipments may drop by 20% to 40 million units. This comes as the Signature 27 debuts, pre-orders begin, and the phone starts to gain traction. What's more, rumors suggest an early foldable launch.
Other popular Android OEMs like Xiaomi are reportedly estimating a 25% reduction in shipments, while Vivo could cut back the most, by 29%, in 2027. Curiously, Google wasn't mentioned in this report, but that doesn't mean things will remain the same.
 



We've already seen the beginnings of Samsung's cutback in October. The company reportedly asked suppliers to reduce smartphone production by 20% to 30% in Q4 2026. The reduction would bring Samsung's 2026 totals closer to 200 million units, down from the projected 270 million. On the other hand, a Counterpoint Research report echoes phone OEM woes.
In Q3, the smartphone market reached its lowest point in 14 years. The market plummeted by 20% YoY. Counterpoint senior analyst Shilpi Jain said, ""The extreme memory shortage is pushing the smartphone market into a more selective phase."" The phone market has suffered this badly since 2012, and we're all looking at how demanding AI has been.
As DRAM and NAND shortages push OEMs into a corner, prices are soaring. As prices soar, ""consumer behavior"" changes. Counterpoint's Jain added, ""Consumers are holding on to their devices longer than estimated, increasingly considering refurbished devices and prioritizing higher-value devices."" Q2 wasn't as drastic, but it still marked a 4% decline in the market, according to Omdia's research.

2,2026-11-25,"The seesaw trading came amid a tumultuous stretch on Wall Street.
The major stock indexes closed mixed on Wednesday, erasing some losses after a sharp decline early in the session as a sell-off hit some top artificial intelligence companies.
The volatile trading came two days after the reinstatement of Jensen Huang as Nvidia CEO rallied markets.
Nvidia dropped nearly 4% in value on Wednesday, extending losses suffered a day earlier and erasing a portion of the gains enjoyed after Huang's reinstatement.
The Dow Jones Industrial Average closed up 0.3%, while the S&P 500 closed down 0.25%, and the tech-heavy Nasdaq declined 0.6%.
The broad market indexes recovered from a slide earlier in the session. Near the outset of trading, the Dow fell more than 1,000 points, while the Nasdaq plunged over 4%.
The dip for AI firms extended well beyond Nvidia. Shares of chip company Micron tumbled 4%. Memory hardware firm Sandisk saw its stock price fall 5%.
Oracle, a cloud-computing and enterprise software firm that offers AI tools, dropped 5%.
On Tuesday, the major stock indexes were mixed after soaring a day earlier in response to Huang's reinstatement.
Those fluctuations followed a selloff last week as tech investors digested fallout from Huang's previous resignation, as well as an initial public offering from artificial intelligence giant Anthropic.
After a decline in early trading on Wednesday, Anthropic closed up 1%, putting its stock price at about $82.79. That figure stands slightly below the stock's IPO price of $83 a share.

            President Donald Trump said Tuesday he would be open to pardoning newly reinstated Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang for any potential crimes — but he added that it likely won’t be needed.
    

            “It’s always an option, I guess, but I don’t think it’ll be necessary,” Trump told the New York Post in an interview when asked about pardoning Huang.
    

            The president also urged Huang to “tell the truth” when facing bipartisan questioning from Congress next week. CNN previously reported that Huang is set to testify behind closed doors on Capitol Hill Tuesday, and he will appear in a public hearing with lawmakers next Thursday.
    

            “I don’t think he needs to take the Fifth. I think it’s an interesting story for people,” Trump said.
    

            “I think I want him to just tell the truth, and I mean, his lawyers are going to have to advise him, but I’ve always found him to be 100% aboveboard, and you know a very smart person,” the president said.
    

Like ‘family businesses’: Inside the Nvidia board that ousted and rehired Jensen Huang


            Trump’s comments come a day after Nvidia’s board brought Huang back as CEO. He returned to the chipmaker’s headquarters to loud cheers and applause from employees.
    

            During Tuesday’s interview, the Post reported, Trump didn’t hide his satisfaction with how the market responded once Nvidia stock rose more than 30% Monday after the board’s decision, checking the latest numbers at the Resolute Desk and pointing out that the stock had recovered.
    

            “That’s not bad, went down a couple of points,” Trump said, “it really picked up at the end.”

4,2026-11-25,"The message from the Trump administration to one of the world’s leading semiconductor companies was clear: Give the US government a chunk of your company. Right now. Or else.
Do not make the “mistake of thinking that this is a negotiation,” Commerce Department lawyer David Shapiro told the chief financial officer of the Intel Corporation in August 2025, according to internal notes from a company call contained in a federal lawsuit. “The President is expecting to announce a deal on these terms on Friday.”
Shapiro acknowledged that “the Board could choose not to accept the offer,” according to a summary of his remarks prepared for Intel’s directors. But the Trump administration official “noted that rejection will have consequences that [Shapiro] cannot predict.”
Days later, the board gave the administration what it wanted. “It is my Great Honor to report that the United States of America now fully owns and controls 10% of INTEL,” Donald Trump posted to his social platform. “The United States paid nothing for these Shares, and the Shares are now valued at approximately $11 Billion Dollars. This is a great Deal for America.”
President Trump was similarly jubilant on Monday, announcing that he had “convinced” Nvidia’s board of directors to rehire chief executive Jensen Huang. The proclamation was a shock. But it wasn’t entirely without precedent.
Fifteen months earlier, the Trump administration had interfered with the leadership decisions at another major chipmaker, court documents allege. Then, as now, allegations swirled that the company’s CEO was too close to China. Then, as now, the Trump administration dealt itself into the company’s financial future.
It’s part of a broader shift within the government in general and the Commerce Department in particular, a former member of that department tells WIRED. Out are old metrics “like creating jobs or the US being more competitive or owning more of the market share,” this ex-Trump administration official says. When dealing with private companies, a single overriding concern is: “Are you going to give back to the government in cash?”
Documents unredacted as part of a shareholder’s lawsuit against Intel’s board show how far the administration has been willing to go to get what it wants. The lawsuit’s allegations were previously reported by WP Intelligence.
“The Board, operating under the shadow of the President’s threats,” the lawsuit alleges, “caved to the government’s extortion.” A second case, asking for internal records, was filed in April by the same shareholder, Richard Paisner, against Nvidia.
“It's just straight-up coercion,” says Scott Lincicome, a vice president at the libertarian Cato Institute. “The company starts making decisions not based on what's best for the company in the short or long term, what's best for shareholders, but what's best for politics and what the government is demanding.”
“That's not what you want from any company,” he adds. “When you're talking about the world's most innovative companies and these kinds of bleeding-edge technologies that have not only huge economic implications but national security ones, then that's a huge problem.”
Representatives for Intel declined to comment for this story. The White House, Commerce Department, and Nvidia did not respond to requests for comment.
The parallels between Nvidia’s government-assisted autogolpe and Intel’s alleged forced sale aren’t exact. In the Intel case, Trump all but accused the company’s boss of being compromised by Beijing; in the Nvidia situation, the president reportedly dismissed concerns from his own intelligence agencies about the executive’s connections to China. But both instances show a growing fusion of private enterprise and public office.
During the Biden administration, Intel won awards for up to $11 billion in government grants and contracts, much of it under the CHIPS Act, which was meant to help build semiconductor manufacturing and assembly plants in America and bolster domestic research.
At the time, there was some grumbling inside the Commerce Department, which administered the CHIPS and Science Act grants, about funding what was seen as a “failing incumbent” in the semiconductor space, says the former official. (Intel had, in recent years, fallen on hard times.) But by March 2025, Intel had received about $2.2 billion of the grants. That’s when President Trump told Congress that the CHIPS Act was “a horrible, horrible thing.” The rest of Intel’s money was put on hold, tied to hitting certain milestones, while the administration said it was renegotiating the deals.
The same month Lip-Bu Tan was hired as Intel’s new CEO. Soon after, his ties to China made him a Trump target, when Reuters reported that Tan had invested in “hundreds of Chinese tech firms,” some with connections to Beijing's military. Tan’s former firm, Cadence Design Systems, pleaded guilty to violating US sanctions by selling semiconductor designs to a restricted Chinese military university, and agreed to pay more than $140 million in fines. Tan was never personally charged, but the violations happened while he was the CEO.
Two weeks later, the president was watching Maria Bartiromo’s show on the Fox Business Network. It mentioned a letter a senator had sent Intel about the China ties. Trump posted, ""The CEO of INTEL is highly CONFLICTED and must resign, immediately. There is no other solution to this problem.""
Tan went to the Oval Office on August 11 to see if there was, in fact, another solution. He got 40 minutes with the president, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, and Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent.
Tan claimed his experience in China was a strength, not a detriment. According to the book Regime Change, Tan argued that there was no way he could’ve known about every one of the deals his many companies made; Trump, who had his own sprawling business empire, surely knew what that was like. Tan promised to give up his holdings in China, according to a letter Tan shared with Intel’s board, and put any proceeds into a blind trust.
“The primary goal was to eliminate any worries about Lip-Bu himself, his Cadence background and his investments with China,” according to a source with knowledge of the matter. But there were major financial concerns for Intel, too. “We had declining revenues and needed money.”
Neither Tan’s letter, excerpted in the complaint, nor Trump’s social media posts praising the CEO after the meeting mentioned anything about giving the government a chunk of the company. Yet Trump later insisted that a deal had been cooked up right then and there. “He came in, he saw me, we talked for a while. I liked him a lot, I thought he was very good,” the president told the Wall Street Journal. “I said, ‘You know what? I think the United States should be given 10% of Intel.’ And he said, ‘I would consider that.’”
“I should’ve asked for more. It was so easy,” Trump later recalled.
According to the court filing’s review of Intel’s internal documents, Shapiro—a former partner at the New York law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz—made a verbal offer on August 18. Ultimately, in exchange for the cut of Intel, the Trump administration would free up the $8.9 billion US government grants that it had already won. They wanted about a tenth of the company’s stock for free, in exchange for Washington keeping the commitment it had already made.
Then came the call in which Shapiro insisted there was no room for negotiation. “The board needs to consider,” he said, according to Intel’s summary of his remarks included in the court documents, the “benefits of having a friend in this administration and what the administration can do to help Intel (including with customers).”
Intel’s board met, and decided they hated the deal. “The economic terms that had been proposed [by] the Administration were not, in their current form, attractive to the company,” minutes of the meeting, reprinted in the lawsuit, noted.
But they went ahead with it anyway. Paisner’s lawyers say in the court documents the board took the offer thrust forward by the administration. Intel’s attorneys disagree, arguing in their motion to dismiss that there was some back-and-forth deal-making, and it made the agreement more favorable to the company. The grants became “immediately available” while “strategically aligning the interests of Intel’s stockholders and the US Government.”
On August 25, Lutnick released a video with Tan, sitting side-by-side on cushioned chairs. “I really look forward to have the US government be my shareholder,” Tan turned to Lutnick and said. “And we can work together and make Intel great again.”
Inside the company, the video was “a surprise to everyone,” according to a source with direct knowledge of the matter. Some employees joked that it was reminiscent of a “hostage video.”
Not long after that, Nvidia—led by Tan’s friend Jensen Huang—made a $5 billion investment in Intel. “I love seeing their stock price go up, making the USA RICHER, AND RICHER,” Trump posted. “Who would not want to make deals like that?”
Deals, plural. On the day Tan was hauled to the Oval Office, Trump announced that his administration had been negotiating separately with Huang. The Nvidia leader was beyond eager to sell more of his semiconductors to China. “And I said, if I'm going to do that, I want you to pay us as a country something,’” Trump said. They settled on 15 percent of all sales of the H20 model chip. Months after that, they cut a second deal: a 25 percent bounty on sales of Nvidia’s even-more advanced H200 chips.
In their lawsuit against Nvidia, Paisner’s attorneys argue the surcharges amount to “unlawful taxes,” and that the board’s “decisions to enter into such agreements are the product of mismanagement and breaches of fiduciary duty.”
In court, NVIDIA’s lawyers insisted that the company “did not enter into any illegal ‘agreement’ with the government but instead engaged with the government and stated that it would comply with federal regulations.” Moreover, the attorneys say Paisner is trying to make a political point, rather than a legal one. “When asked about his purpose in bringing this action,” they write, “Paisner was candid: ‘It’s to make a public point of how NVIDIA conducts itself.’”
The attorneys wrote this in May, months before Nvidia’s and Huang’s recent drama. Last week, board chair Harvey Jones said on the company’s quarterly earnings call that it did “not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO,” citing his “disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company’s code of conduct, standards, and cultural norms.” (Not to mention mounting evidence that the company’s most sophisticated chips were making their way to China, in violation of US law.) But, according to The New York Times, after days of pressure from top administration officials, the company and the government essentially struck a deal Sunday night to reinstate Huang.
“Don’t think for a second that politics ever play a role in this company, not even a little bit,” Huang said during his first company-wide meeting after rejoining the company, WIRED reported. “We are the most apolitical company. I don’t love that we talk about politics here. Take it home. I love that we talk about work here.”
Additional reporting by Lauren Goode.

Watch: Why has Poundland collapsed into administration?
Poundland has collapsed into administration, putting 11,000 jobs at risk, after its owners failed to find a buyer to keep it in business.
The retailer's shops and distribution centres will remain open and continue to trade during the administration process with the business likely to be broken up and some of its stores sold off to rival retailers.
Poundland's US owners held talks with several bidders over a last-minute rescue deal, but no agreement was reached.
Poundland boss Barry Williams said in a note to staff that the board had ""no alternative but to take this decision"", but was making it with ""sincere sadness and intense regret"".
He said the US owners Gordon Brothers had removed ""all borrowing facilities and access to our bank accounts"" on 27 October, and adequate funds had not been released since then to keep the business solvent.
""Under these circumstances, it is impossible for the board of directors to see how Poundland can continue to operate as a going concern,"" he added.
Kelvin Blake, pictured at a protest in Walsall, has worked for Poundland for 25 years
Kelvin Blake, 62, who has worked for the firm for 25 years, and whose partner has been there 29 years, said he was feeling ""down, depressed"", and because of his age, feared he would not find a job elsewhere.
""We didn't want it, but it's come so now we've just got to face it and see what the future holds.
""My income's gone,"" he added. ""Obviously, we'll be working until we're told to leave.""
Prime Minister Andy Burnham said Poundland was ""part of the fabric of high streets and communities up and down the country"".
He said the news of it going into administration just weeks before Christmas would be ""deeply concerning for workers and their families"".
He added: ""While it is worrying, going into administration does give other potential buyers the opportunity to engage and we will work hard with all the relevant parties to ensure the best possible outcome.""
Alix Partners, which has been appointed as administrator, said they would look at all options for the business, ""including sales of the business and its assets, either in whole or part"".
""We appreciate this is a deeply concerning time for all involved - customers, suppliers and, in particular, Poundland colleagues,"" they said.
Gordon Brothers has been criticised over how it handled a potential sale of Poundland, with accusations the investment firm hoped to profit from selling off the chain's 600 shops and stock piecemeal after running it into the ground.
Gordon Brothers, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied such claims.
On the funding question, Gordon Brothers said it had loaned the firm Â£85m over a year to help save it from insolvency.
But insiders critical of the US firm have said it stands to make its loans back and more from the insolvency process.
Previously, Gordon Brothers netted millions from liquidating the stock of fashion retailer LK Bennett, and in insolvency fees.
Labour MP Liam Byrne told the BBC the House of Commons' Business, Innovation, Science and Trade Committee will recall Gordon Brothers before Parliament - two weeks after Gordon Brothers representatives last appeared at the committee to answer questions about Poundland, and where MPs said they were ""deeply concerned"" about the business.
Byrne said: ""I am really worried that a 'greed is good' approach to business by its owners has forced [Poundland] into an administration."" 
He added the committee wants to know ""how much money they're ripping out and crucially, how do we make sure, as a country, that we don't have this kind of scenario unfold again"".
What's gone wrong at Poundland?
Gordon Brothers met Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds on Monday to update him on the progress.
The firm said on Wednesday that despite the Poundland management bringing about a turnaround over the past year, the firm was ""still vulnerable"" to inflation and any downturn in sales or profit margin, and it had faced ""several years of instability"".
A spokesperson added the firm's ""thoughts are with Poundland colleagues"".
It said it had stepped in to take over the retailer in 2025 when it was struggling, and when ""no-one else was willing to finance the business"".
One of the central stumbling blocks in negotiations to keep the business afloat was a shareholder loan Gordon Brothers inherited when it bought the business for less than Â£1 last year.
Gordon Brothers was owed Â£22.5m of that loan, while the previous owner Pepco was owed Â£7.5m.
Gordon Brothers rejected various offers to buy the business as a going concern with deals to pay off that loan.
Irish firm Martin Property Group offered to pay off Â£5m of the loan up front, and defer the rest of the payments to 2030.
London-based Xyra group offered Â£12.5m up front but ran out of time to get a deal over the line.
German investment firm Zachert Private Equity is also understood to have made a late offer.
All of these were rejected.
The crisis at Poundland really came to a head over the past few weeks after Gordon Brothers restricted access to funds for day-to-day operations.
This meant that stock at the retailer was not being replenished at the necessary pace in the vital run-up to the festive period.

9,2026-11-25,"A Russian MiG-31 military jet (file photo)
Ukraine has said it damaged five Russian military aircraft in an attack on Russia's Savasleyka airfield, 300km (185 miles) east of Moscow.
Three MiG-31 and two Su-34s aircraft were hit, Kyiv's General Staff said, adding that the extent of the damage was still being assessed but that ""there may be even better news to come"". It did not elaborate on what weapons it had used to hit the airfield. 
Russia has not commented on the strikes. 
The airfield at Savasleyka, in Russia's Nizhny Novgorod region, has been a target for Ukraine for several years. It hosts aircraft used by Russia to launch hypersonic missiles and glide bombs. 
The take-off of MiG-31s in particular frequently triggers nationwide alerts across Ukraine, as the Kinzhal missiles they carry have been known to hit large infrastructure and military targets and are difficult to shoot down.
Overnight, Kyiv also said it hit two oil refineries in Russia's Samara region some 800km (500 miles) from the front line â the latest in a series of long-range strikes which have dented Moscow's oil processing capacity.
""Did you repair them? We'll hit them again,"" the defence ministry in Kyiv said.
Samara region governor Vyacheslav Fedorishchev said the night-time attacks had been ""a true ordeal"", with more than 145 drones shot down. One person was killed and several towns experienced power outages, Fedorishchev said.
Kyiv also said it had hit a radar system as well as fuel and logistics depots across Ukrainian territories currently occupied by Russia.
""Without fuel, military vehicles cannot move; without ammunition, artillery falls silent,"" the defence ministry said, adding that every successful hit meant Moscow could carry out fewer strikes.
Kyiv has developed a thriving weapons industry and Ukrainian strikes on Russian targets well beyond the front line have become commonplace in the years since Moscow launched its full-scale invasion.
On Tuesday, President Vladimir Putin called for a tightening of security measures across the country's energy facilities. 
But he also argued that Russia had managed to stabilise its domestic fuel market without suffering ""critical damage"" and added that Moscow would ""inevitably"" retaliate against Kyiv.
Ukraine continues to come under daily attack. Ten people were killed across several regions between 24 and 25 November, while thousands are without power after Russian strikes on energy infrastructure.

Denys Sienik took to X Wednesday to explain how the company’s seemingly inexplicable regional internet failures and Starshield terminal restrictions have only caused Ukraine more wounds in its ongoing war against the Russian invasion.
“The Starshield restrictions in Ukraine have created a vicious circle: Neither of the key stakeholders has been able to clarify who made the decision to impose these limitations,” Sienik wrote.
“To be frank and straightforward: Ukrainians already face enough life-threatening challenges as a result of Russia’s ongoing aggression,” he continued. “We cannot afford to accept the disruption of a critical service such as Starshield as just another episode and simply move on. Every day these limitations remain in place creates real risks and potentially devastating consequences for people on the ground.”
Ukraine has suffered from several intelligence gaps since last month, when Elon Musk’s satellite-supplied internet service suddenly dropped its connection over the country as Russia barraged Kyiv with missiles. Musk initially blamed the lapse on the decisions of the U.S. military and Donald Trump, but then Trump also refused to take responsibility. 
Musk eventually confessed to slowing down the region’s Starlink connection. He claimed in an X post on October 24 that Ukraine had violated Starlink’s terms of service by using the civilian variation of SpaceX’s internet connection to attack Russia, even though Ukraine’s defenses were attempting to ward off Russian missiles at the time.

A major misstep by the Justice Department has forced the nation’s top law enforcement agency to grovel before a federal judge.
An errata court filing submitted to the D.C. District Court Tuesday by U.S. Attorney Jeanine Pirro proved to be a major embarrassment for the DOJ, as the agency was forced to explicitly apologize for giving what a lawyer for the House of Representatives described as a “categorically false” assessment of his comments about the predawn raid on Representative Ilhan Omar’s home.
The issue began last month—before Homeland Security agents raided the Minnesota Democrat’s Washington home on October 12 in an attempt to uncover evidence that her age did not match her original citizenship paperwork.
Search warrant details publicized Monday by the chief judge of the D.C. District Court, James E. Boasberg, revealed a grave error. At the time, the DOJ told the Magistrate Judge Matthew Sharbaugh—who eventually approved the warrant—that House general counsel Matthew Berry had effectively signed off on the raid by agreeing with the administration’s view of the Constitution’s speech or debate clause, insofar that lawmakers’ protections “do not extend to a member’s private residence.” 
But that was “categorically false,” according to Berry. On Tuesday, Berry said that he never intended to be involved in the case, since the House general counsel’s office does not serve as criminal defense counsel. He also referred to an October 11 email he sent the department, indicating that he had informed the DOJ of his position ahead of the raid on Omar’s house.
Hours later, Pirro noted in her errata that the detail “requires correction,” claiming that DOJ prosecutors had misinterpreted Berry’s phrasing.
“The United States’ counsel misunderstood that to be drawing a distinction based on Speech or Debate Clause concerns, but it was actually premised only on House equities regarding the place of a search, and the House General Counsel took no position on the scope of Speech or Debate Clause protections,” Pirro’s filing stated.

13,2026-11-25,"The Fed will issue a decision on interest rates early next month.
The Federal Reserve's preferred gauge of inflation climbed in October, according to a government report issued on Wednesday. The measure is sure to draw attention from central bankers as they weigh a possible rate increase early next month.
The Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE) showed annual inflation climbed from 3.6% in September to 3.8% in October. The reading matched economists' expectations.
That figure for October marked the highest annual PCE in five months, putting inflation nearly two percentage points above the Fed's target level of 2%.
The report arrived weeks after a separate inflation measure, the Consumer Price Index (CPI), showed a larger jump in prices in October than economists had expected.
Fuel prices have accounted for a sizable share of overall inflation since the outbreak of the Iran war in late February set off a global oil supply shortage.
A cooldown in oil prices since the midterm elections has put downward pressure on gasoline prices, however, leaving the national average price of a gallon of gas at $3.58 as of Wednesday, AAA data showed. That figure amounted to a decline of about 15% from a month earlier.
The relief at the pump in recent weeks did not appear in the report on Wednesday, since it detailed prices in October.
The fresh data is sure to garner attention from the Fed at a delicate moment, when policymakers are weighing a rate increase that could help tackle inflation but risks a slowdown in hiring.
A stronger-than-expected jobs report earlier this month may ease possible concern among central bankers about a cooldown for the labor market.
Economic growth has also proven resilient, displaying better-than-expected performance in a quarterly report on gross domestic product released last month.
If the Fed were to issue a rate increase at its next meeting in December, the decision would mark the central bank's second rate hike in four months, after it had gone three years without raising rates.
The odds of a quarter-point rate hike at the Fed's next meeting are pegged at about 97%, according to CME Group's FedWatch Tool, a measure of market sentiment.
Days before the midterm elections, Fed policymakers opted to hold interest rates steady as they awaited further economic data. Still, Fed Chair Kevin Warsh vowed to cool off inflation.
""It’s our responsibility to get inflation down,"" Warsh said at a press conference in Washington, D.C., at the time.
Wall Street will also take notice of the PCE report after a volatile stretch of trading. The major stock indexes were mixed on Tuesday after soaring a day earlier in response to an announcement from Nvidia reinstating Jensen Huang as CEO.
Those fluctuations followed a selloff last week as tech investors digested fallout from Huang's previous resignation as well as an initial public offering from artificial intelligence giant Anthropic.

14,2026-11-25,"Black Friday hasnât always been about filling shopping bags to the brim and taking advantage of online deals.
According to History, the first Black Friday had nothing to do with clothing discounts or shoppers fighting over the latest retail items. The date wasnât even close to Thanksgiving.
So, what really happened on the first âBlack Friday,â and how did it evolve into a shopping holiday that spurs Americans to camp outside stores and relentlessly add to their online shopping carts?Â 
Letâs flip back a page or two in American history and see what we can find!Â 
The first Black Friday was on September 24, 1869, otherwise known as the crash of the U.S. gold market.Â 
Two devious Wall Street financiers, Jay Gould and Jim Fisk, let greed consume them and decided to buy as much gold as they could get their hands on, drive up its price, and sell it for a greater profit. This wasnât the first time the deceitful pair had resorted to such schemes. According to PBS, one year earlier, they âused stock fraud and bribery to keep Cornelius Vanderbilt from taking control of the Erie Railroad.â
Connected to President Ulysses S. Grant through his brother-in-law, Abel Rathbone Corbin, Gould and Fisk put a bug in the Commander-in-Chiefâs ear, persuading him not to sell more gold so its value would rise. With Corbinâs approval, General Daniel Butterfield became assistant treasurer of the United States and promised to keep Gould and Fisk privy to the government's gold transactions.Â 
When Grant discovered what Corbin, and therefore Butterfield, was doing behind the scenes, he âordered the sale of $4,000,000 in government gold.â This decreased the price of gold from $160 to $133 and sent the stock market tumbling down. Investors immediately panicked, brokerage houses went bankrupt, and the market fell 20 percent, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of New York.
Investors who had borrowed money to buy gold at the higher price suddenly couldnât repay their loans, and Corbin was among them. Gould, however, was savvy enough to sell his gold before the collapse. He ended up controlling the Union Pacific Railroad, Western Union Telegraph Company, and the Manhattan Elevated Railroad. His partner, whom he hadnât told of his sales, wasnât so lucky.
Three years after the first Black Friday, Fisk was shot and killed by financier Edward Stokes, ironically, during a dispute that involved money. Neither Gould nor Fisk ever served time behind bars for the scandal that caused the first Black Friday.
The term was reborn in the 1950s, when tourists traveled to Philadelphia in droves on the Friday after Thanksgiving for the annual ArmyâNavy football game. Police officers worked extra shifts to manage the crowds, according to History. Shoplifters contributed to the chaos by stealing from stores, demanding more attention from law enforcement.
From then on, Philadelphia referred to the day as âBlack Friday.â In 1961, local officials tried to rename it âBig Friday,â but it didnât catch on. Then, in the 1980s, retailersÂ reinterpreted Black Friday as a day when stores moved from red (operating at a loss) to black (making a profit) through extensive holiday discounts.Â 
Though we've held on to this version of Black Friday for decades, its story began with the U.S. gold market crash of 1869 and Philadelphiaâs hectic ArmyâNavy weekend in the â50s.Â 
Today, the beloved shopping holiday isnât even contained to a single day. It now spans online and in-store sales that begin weeks in advance and continue for days afterward. In 2026, we can be thankful that Black Friday has nothing to do with stock market crashes and lawless shopping chaos. 
Happy shopping!Â

You can get the Legion Go S with 1TB of storage for your Steam games at Lenovo, where the handheld is down to $850, from $1,608.
Writer
Writer

				Edited by Corinne Reichert 
November 25, 2026, 1:42 pm ET
Save $758: You can get Lenovo’s Legion Go S gaming handheld with SteamOS for $850, down from $1,608 in Lenovo’s Black Friday sale. For that price, you’ll get the configuration with an AMD Ryzen Z1 Extreme processor, 32GB of RAM and a 1TB SSD, which will give you room for a lot of games. Lenovo has labeled the deal a doorbuster and is limiting orders to five units, so you should act fast if you want it, as you may not be able to get this price through Black Friday on Nov. 27.

			Lenovo/CNET		

			Get it now		
CNET’s Lori Grunin prefers the Legion Go S with SteamOS over the Steam Deck OLED. You’ll get an 8-inch display on the Legion Go S, which is larger than the display on the Steam Deck OLED. The display can refresh at up to 120Hz, so fast-moving games will look smoother. This one runs SteamOS, which will give you a console-like interface and quick access to your Steam library.
You’ll get a Ryzen Z1 Extreme processor and 32GB of RAM with this configuration, which will help with more demanding games. Lenovo is offering free delivery and a price match guarantee, and you can return the Legion Go S until Jan. 27. You can also grab the Legion Go S carry case at checkout, where the case is down to $8, from $20. We don’t know how long Lenovo’s stock will last, so you should order soon if you want the Legion Go S at $850.
You would have paid $900 for this Legion Go S configuration at launch, and as much as $1,608 after that as gaming component costs rise. At $850, you’ll be paying $50 less than the launch price. 
 If the Legion Go S isn’t the right handheld for you, you can pick from a few alternatives from CNET’s list of the best handheld gaming consoles.
More from CNET

			Nintendo/CNET		

			Get it now		
The Nintendo Switch 2 is our pick for the best overall gaming handheld, with a CNET score of 8.5 out of 10. The Switch 2 has a slim design with a large 1080p display, and the controllers attach magnetically. We got less battery life from the Switch 2 than from the original Switch in our testing. Nintendo only lets you play games bought on game cards or through the eShop, so you won’t be able to bring your Steam library to the Switch 2.

			Valve/CNET		

			Get it now		
Valve’s Steam Deck OLED uses the same SteamOS software as the Legion Go S. The Steam Deck OLED has an HDR OLED display, and we liked the controls and the improved haptics in our testing. Valve is selling the Steam Deck OLED for $789, which is $61 less than the Legion Go S. The Steam Deck OLED has a smaller screen than the Legion Go S, though, so you’ll have to decide if the $61 saving makes up for the difference in size.

			Asus/CNET		

			Get it now		
The ROG Xbox Ally X runs Windows, so you can install games from multiple game stores without any workarounds. The grips are larger than the ones on most handhelds, which can make the ROG Xbox Ally X more comfortable to hold. We found the performance to be excellent in lighter games, but Windows can sometimes slow the handheld down. At $1,000 at Best Buy, the ROG Xbox Ally X is $150 more expensive than the Legion Go S.
It depends on the handheld. Lenovo has cut the Legion Go S with SteamOS to $850, from $1,608, for Black Friday. But we haven’t found Black Friday discounts on the Nintendo Switch 2, the Steam Deck OLED or the Asus ROG Xbox Ally X yet. You’ll find more discounts in other categories right now, including Best Buy’s Black Friday OLED TV deals and our Black Friday headphone deals.
You likely won’t gain much by waiting. Lenovo has labeled the $850 price a doorbuster, and we don’t know how long the stock will last. Best Buy’s Black Friday sale recently had the same model for $901, which is $51 more than Lenovo’s price. Lenovo is also offering a price match guarantee and extended returns through Jan. 27, in case you find a lower price during Amazon’s Black Friday sale or Walmart’s Black Friday sale.
CNET Senior Editor Lori Grunin prefers the Legion Go S with SteamOS, which has a larger display and a different control layout. The Steam Deck OLED is still our pick for the best PC handheld on our list of the best handheld gaming consoles, and it’s the one I would buy myself. The Steam Deck OLED has an HDR OLED display, while the Legion Go S has an LCD screen. The Legion Go S and the Steam Deck OLED both use SteamOS, so you should be able to play most of the same Steam games on either handheld.

16,2026-11-25,"The cost of turkey is driving the cost of Thanksgiving dinner higher this year.
A 16-pound frozen turkey will set you back $23.68 on average this year, according to the American Farm Bureau Federation. That's 10% higher than in 2025.
That's one of the big reasons that a 10-person Thanksgiving dinner will cost 6% more this year — about $5.86 a head, the Federation said.
For many shoppers, a more expensive Thanksgiving turkey caps a year of price increases.
Consumers have paid more for a range of goods this year, both due to higher tariffs on imports as well as sharply higher fuel prices in the wake of the US and Israel starting a war with Iran. Voter frustration about those rising prices helped Democrats secure one of their biggest midterm election victories in decades.
This year's rise in Thanksgiving dinner costs has its origin elsewhere: Disease.
For over a decade, US turkey farmers have been battling highly pathogenic avian influenza, or HPAI, said Russ Whitman, director of market reporting at commodity intelligence platform Expana. Bird flu has hit other parts of the food chain, including eggs.
The latest spike in cases came in 2022. Since then, HPAI cases have declined, but the disease hasn't gone away. That means fewer turkeys and higher prices, Whitman said.
""The turkey industry was devastated, and that led to a several-year cycle of not having turkeys rebuilt,"" he said.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Wholesale frozen turkey prices, or what retailers pay for turkeys, stood at $1.95 a pound as of October — a record high, according to Expana.
Potatoes, another key Thanksgiving ingredient, are on track for one of the ""smallest crops on record"" this year after bad weather and reduced crop acreage, Expana Market Reporter Craig Elliott said. Prices for wheat and cane sugar are also pushing Thanksgiving prices up.
Other Thanksgiving foods are cheaper than a year ago, Expana found. The wholesale price for ham, a popular second-choice protein on Thanksgiving tables, was 32% lower last month at 69 cents a pound, for instance. And Expana's Pecan Pie Index fell 9.3% this year as cheaper butter and vanilla more than offset more expensive sugar and pecans.
For many shoppers, though, the rising prices of turkey and other Thanksgiving staples might not matter.
Most major retailers signed contracts to buy their turkeys earlier this year when prices were lower, Whitman said. And many shoppers are buying the turkeys at an even steeper discount through offerings such as Walmart's $35 Thanksgiving meal deal or 39-cent-a-pound specials when they spend a certain amount at some grocery stores.
That means many retailers are willing to absorb higher turkey prices to get customers to shop in their stores, Whitman said.
""Any way you slice it, it's a loss leader,"" he said.

18,2026-11-25,"Markets fall in Japan, South Korea, Taiwan and Hong Kong in latest tech-led sell-off.
Save
Share
Asian stock markets have fallen sharply as doubts continue to swirl over the AI boom, reversing a rally led by Jensen Huang’s return as CEO of Nvidia.
South Korea’s stock market led the sell-off, with the Kospi plunging 6.2 percent.
Taiwan’s Taiex closed down 4.8 percent, after earlier falling more than 8 percent.
Japan’s Nikkei 225 dropped 2.8 percent, while Hong Kong’s Hang Seng Index shed morning gains to finish 1.1 percent in the red.
Tech stocks linked to the AI trade suffered some of the steepest losses.
In Japan, semiconductor equipment makers Tokyo Electron and Advantest dived 12.5 percent and 12.9 percent, respectively.
South Korea’s Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix, the top two global memory chip makers, sank 9.9 percent and 11.1 percent, respectively.
In Taiwan, Apple supplier Hon Hai Precision Industry, also known as Foxconn, slid 8.8 percent, while top contract chip maker Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC) closed down 3.6 percent.
The retreat comes after Asian markets surged on Tuesday following Huang’s surprise reinstatement at the helm of chip giant Nvidia after an intervention by US President Donald Trump.
Nvidia’s reappointment of Huang, who resigned amid claims he was romantically involved with a woman suspected of working with Chinese intelligence, sent the chip giant’s New York-listed shares 31.5 percent higher on Monday, the largest single-day gain in US stock market history.
Wall Street saw modest losses on Tuesday after the previous session’s buying spree, with the S&P 500 falling 0.5 percent and the Nasdaq composite down 1.5 percent.
Markets have seen wild swings over the past month amid the Nvidia scandal and expectations that Democrats in Washington, DC, will push for greater regulation of AI following their sweeping victories in the US midterm elections.
Those headwinds have added to existing concerns that tech firms may not be able to sustain their multitrillion-dollar investments underpinning the AI boom.
Big Tech’s debt-fuelled splurge has stirred intense debate about whether it is investing in a future gold mine or a massive stock market bubble.
Global AI-related investment is forecast to reach $2.7 trillion this year and $3.6 trillion in 2027, according to technology insights company Gartner.

23,2026-11-25,"
							By 

	Jack Crosbie


	Earlier this year, before coming on to Rolling Stone’s staff full time, I sold some stock. Back in the early 2000s, my grandmother had willed me a handful of shares of Disney stock, which sat around appreciating value and were worth a few grand. I told myself I was selling off the Disney stock for ethical reasons — many places I wrote for as a freelancer asked employees to sign contracts that attest that they won’t buy, sell, or hold stock in individual companies they cover. Really, though, I just needed the cash. 

Donald Trump, however, has no such qualms. Nor does he really need a little extra cash. In his time as president, he has made billions of dollars, some of it through the sale of stock in companies that his policies have directly affected.

	This week, after exerting a considerable amount of political pressure, Trump convinced the board of Nvidia, the most valuable company in the world, to reinstate its disgraced CEO Jensen Huang after an absurd scandal that revealed Huang was potentially complicit in helping one of his mistresses buy up advanced Nvidia computer chips on behalf of the Chinese government. Huang’s ouster — and the whole selling super computer parts to a Chinese spy thing — had tanked Nvidia’s stock in recent weeks, dragging most of Wall Street down with it, which was not something that Trump wanted. But now, Huang is back as CEO, and the numbers are once again going up — including Trump’s own. According to financial disclosures from September, Trump owns hundreds of thousands of shares in Nvidia — which could have gained as much as $15 million in value in the past 48 hours alone, since Huang’s reinstatement.

	










	The scale of Trump’s corruption and grift in office is something we have truly never seen before, but elected officials making big money off the stock market is nothing new. There are no provisions prohibiting politicians from investing in private companies — only limp guidelines passed in 2012 that force them to disclose their positions online and promise not to use privileged information. Shockingly, that hasn’t stopped many of them from taking advantage of their position for years. The numbers don’t lie: There are even indexes that often out-perform the S&P 500 and multiple major hedge funds just by mirroring the exact trades that former House Speaker Nancy Pelosi makes.

	











	
		Editor’s picks
	
	











	
	
		
					The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far		
	














	
	
		
					The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time		
	














	
	
		
					The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time		
	














	
	
		
					100 Best Movies of the 21st Century		
	








	Trump, of course, has taken this to its extreme end, at times appearing to manipulate markets on behalf of his friends and political allies, while enriching himself through everything from stock sales to pump-and-dump crypto scams. In 2025 alone, the president’s annual earnings were more than $2.2 billion, which makes Pelosi’s millions look like the take from a podunk coin laundromat. But ultimately, the discrepancy is beside the point. Why do we let any of these people trade stocks at all? Why is a cub reporter at a newspaper held to a higher standard than someone holding federal elected office? Why are members of Congress allowed to pass legislation pertaining to companies they have a financial stake in? Why is the president of the United States allowed to wheel and deal on Wall Street as he makes decisions that can turn the global economy on a dime?

	In a functioning democracy, this would all be banned. And I mean all of it: a total ban on owning individual stock for any elected representative. Hell, ban their staff and consultants too, but start with the electeds. The point of representative democracy is for the public to appoint someone to serve as their avatar in government. That person’s job, then, becomes representing their constituents. They should have no other occupation, no other outside interests. I’d go as far as to say that they should have to sell off any equity in businesses they themselves started (and get rid of this nonsense of “blind trusts”). If they must hold stocks, they should have to dump their portfolio into a mutual fund or market index, and be prohibited from buying or selling at all until they leave public office. These people are supposed to work for us.





	
		Related Content
	
	






	
	
		
					Thomas Markle Indicted on Charges of Having Child Sex Abuse Material		
	









	
	
		
					Cher’s Victory in Royalties War Under Fire as Mary Bono Pushes for Reversal		
	









	
	
		
					Hollywood Heavyweights Urge Dems to Dump Schumer		
	









	
	
		
					Nick Carter Loses Bid to Dismiss Sexual Battery Claim From Melissa Schuman		
	








	That doesn’t mean the job is easy. Freshmen members of Congress who don’t come from money often have to get roommates when they come to D.C., because their base salary of $174,00 a year (in the House) can’t keep up with the cost of living their new life in politics. So increase their pay! I have no problem with every member of Congress, every major elected official, making a salary of a million dollars a year. That’s barely more than the league minimum for a rookie in the NFL, and I’m pretty comfortable saying that more people depend on the job performance of the former Speaker of the House than they do on the quality of the third-string offensive lineman for the San Francisco 49ers (no offense to Enrique Cruz Jr., who has a bright career ahead of him). Pay them all a million bucks, two million bucks, whatever — but strip them of everything else. 

	










	It’s clear, too, that the American people are fed up with this. In exit polls following the 2026 midterms, 90 percent of voters said that corruption was a crucial issue to them. Many of the country’s political failings stem from a lack of trust — in leaders, in institutions, in the building blocks of society and social contract. It’s hard to rebuild those with leaders who are so blatantly helping themselves to a bigger share of every pie that comes across their table. Democrats, newly poised to take charge of the House and Senate, have pledged to bring their investigative might down on Trump and his children, but my fear is that the rot will remain even if the most corrupt president in history is taken down.





	
		Trending Stories
	
	






	
	
		
					Duran Duran Guitarist Andy Taylor Dead at 65		
	









	
	
		
					Duran Duran’s Andy Taylor Was the Wildest of the Wild Boys		
	









	
	
		
					The Country Song About Losing Your Virginity That Became an Enduring Hit		
	









	
	
		
					Thank You, Jensen Huang		
	








	Trump is a particularly egregious offender, but he didn’t create the system he exploited. There are 15 members of Congress with net worths in the hundreds of millions, and dozens more with millions in play. As the system is currently constructed, they’re all going to continue trading stock — an act that benefits no one but themselves. Hakeem Jeffries, Pelosi’s assumed successor as House Speaker, has pledged to make banning stock trades a priority next year, but it’s unclear how the push will play out with Trump unlikely to sign such a ban — at least as long as it includes the president — and if Democrats will be as motivated to pass it when he leaves office.

	Running for office should be a selfless act. The point should be to subsume your own sense of self into the collective will of the people who elected you. If you want to get rich in America, go ahead and get rich, but know that we elect our leaders to serve, not rule. It’s time we started making sure they do so with both clear eyes and empty pockets.

This guy can’t be serious man 😭🙏 https://t.co/nPK9ryxhGj
The Pershing Square chief and the Icahn Enterprises chairman famously locked horns over Herbalife, a nutrition-supplement company, in the mid-2010s.
Ackman began shorting Herbalife in late 2012, betting $1 billion that the stock would go to zero. He also launched a campaign to convince regulators and investors that the multi-level marketer was a pyramid scheme.
Icahn took the other side of that trade and went on to become Herbalife's largest shareholder. The two Wall Street titans memorably clashed on live television in 2013, trading a barrage of insults, with Icahn labeling Ackman a ""crybaby,"" and Ackman calling him a ""bully.""
The pair cooled things down, hugging onstage at a conference in 2014. Icahn said in 2016 that he and Ackman were on ""friendly terms,"" but when it came to Herbalife, they had ""agreed to disagree (vehemently) on this subject.""
However, the two men have butted heads in recent years. Ackman wrote in an X post in 2023: ""Over his storied career, Icahn has made many enemies. I don't know that he has any real friends.""
Icahn fired back in a comment to CNBC's Scott Wapner: ""Taking advice from Ackman concerning short selling is like taking advice from Napoleon or the German General Staff on how to invade Russia.""

Foldables are sticking around—especially wide foldables. However, let's not forget about the ambitious ""TriFold."" Rumors suggest Samsung's working on a sequel for a couple of familiar markets.

Tech > Services & Software > Cybersecurity
The files included passwords and data that can keep you signed in. The number of people affected is unknown.

				Edited by Corinne Reichert 
November 24, 2026, 7:15 pm ET
A database containing about 2 billion login records for sites across the internet was accessible online without a password, security researcher Jeremiah Fowler said in a report published Tuesday by ExpressVPN. The collection included usernames and passwords tied to email, social media, shopping and financial services. Among the files was an ad offering access to email accounts sorted by geography.
The 2 billion figure is an estimate and not representative of the number of people affected, since some accounts may appear more than once and one person can have dozens of logins saved on a single device. Fowler reported the database to its hosting provider, which restricted public access several days later. It’s unclear how long the files had been exposed or who else may have accessed them.
Fowler’s estimate of about 2 billion login records applies to the database he found. Wired put the total at more than 3.5 billion by including a separate database that security firm UpGuard disclosed last week. That database held about 1.64 billion records, including Chinese national ID numbers, phone numbers and school enrollment records.
More from CNET
Some of the files Fowler reviewed appeared to be from infostealers — malicious software that collects saved passwords and other data from infected devices. Once that information is collected, brokers can buy it in bulk, sort it by country or account type and resell it in smaller batches. A buyer interested in email accounts in a particular country can pay for that slice of the data. The people selling it can make money from the same records more than once.
Fowler saw signs that the files came from multiple sources, but couldn’t determine who assembled the collection or whether it was used for sales, research or another purpose.
I saw a version of that business in my reporting on the dark web for CNET. On Genesis Market, buyers could shop for stolen accounts by location and category, including banking, email and social media. By the time authorities disrupted the marketplace in 2023, it had offered access to data from more than 1.5 million compromised computers, according to the Justice Department.
Genesis also sold browser cookies that could help someone access an account without entering a password. Fowler found similar sign-in data, known as session tokens, in the exposed collection. It’s the same kind of data that lets you close a browser tab and come back later without signing in again.
You can look up your email address in Have I Been Pwned to see whether it appears in a known data exposure. A clean result only tells you what’s in the service’s index.
If you see an unfamiliar login or suspect your device is infected, use a different, trusted device to change the affected password and sign out of other sessions. Start with your email if several accounts may be affected, since that’s where password reset links often go. Replace reused passwords with unique ones, use a password manager to keep track of them and turn on two-factor authentication where it’s available.
Identity theft protection services monitor for signs that your personal information has been exposed or misused. In a leak like this one, an alert about an exposed password can tell you which account to secure, especially if you’ve reused that password elsewhere. Aura is one of the services we tested for CNET’s guide to the best identity theft protection services.


		© 2026 CNET, a Ziff Davis company. All rights reserved. 	"
27,2026-11-25,"We may finally find out who paid for early access to President Donald Trump’s Truth Social posts.
On Tuesday, Southern District of New York Judge J. Paul Oetken granted limited discovery on Trump Media to The Intercept and the Freedom of the Press Foundation as part of their lawsuit against the media company, allowing them access to who subscribes to Truth API—Trump’s blatant pay-to-play scheme in which Trump Media & Technology sells special access to Trump’s market-moving online rants, allowing Wall Street traders an advantage over the general public with regard to reacting to Trump-based market fluctuations.
“Plaintiffs may seek discovery from Trump Media with respect to: (1) the operation of Truth Social API, including but not limited to the marginal speed advantage and archival access obtained by subscribers; (2) the uptake in membership of Truth Social API by other media companies; and (3) communications between Trump Media and Defendants,” Oetken’s order reads.
In August, Trump Media revealed it had 10 trading firms as subscribers, paying $60,000 to $100,000 per month. Trump and aide Natalie Harp were sued by watchdog groups American Oversight and Campaign for Accountability in September. Truth API has also been sued by the city of San Francisco. 
Moneymakers and stars never face their comeuppance in Donald Trump’s world, because, as the president explained in a 2005 hot-mic moment, “when you’re a star … you can do anything.”
The president has offered to pardon newly returned Nvidia chief Jensen Huang—even though he previously insisted Huang had done nothing wrong to prompt his initial ouster from the company.
Trump told the New York Post on Tuesday that a pardon is “always an option.”
Huang resumed his roles as Nvidia’s president, chief executive officer, and member of the board on Monday. The reappointment came mere days after Huang had been tossed out of the company that he co-founded over revelations of his numerous extramarital affairs, the most dubious of which involved a Chinese businesswoman whom U.S. intelligence agencies identified as a spy and a state asset. The paramour, former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang (no relation of Jensen’s), had reportedly been in contact with an officer from China’s Ministry of State Security throughout her affair with the Nvidia chief.
Huang is scheduled to appear before the House Foreign Affairs Committee on Tuesday and then the Senate Banking Committee two days later to discuss the matter. In his interview with the Post, Trump said he didn’t believe Huang would need to invoke the Fifth Amendment to avoid incriminating himself in the upcoming congressional hearings.
“I don’t think he needs to take the Fifth. I think it’s an interesting story for people,” Trump told the Post. “I think I want him to just tell the truth, and I mean, his lawyers are going to have to advise him, but I’ve always found him to be 100 percent aboveboard, and you know, a very smart person.”
Earlier this week, the president revealed that he personally vouched to Nvidia’s board that Huang was not a national security threat. 
Nvidia shares spiraled in the weeks after Huang was ousted from the company, rattling the U.S. economy and Trump’s personal stock portfolio with it: The president’s 2026 financial disclosures revealed that he purchased between $1 million and $5 million of Nvidia stock earlier this year. As of September 30, Trump was believed to have 370,000 shares in Nvidia, according to a review by the Financial Times. Huang’s unexpected reappointment sent the company’s stock skyrocketing, jumping by 31.5 percent and adding some $950 billion to the company’s overall value. Trump, for his part, made up to $15 million, the Financial Times reported.
Markwayne Mullin is losing the White House.
New reporting from The Washington Post contends that President Trump’s second homeland security secretary is seen as a “liability” by high-ranking officials in the Trump administration, particularly after his awkward, non-reassuring handling of the Federal Aviation Administration’s nationwide grounding of flights earlier this month—baselessly suggesting that AI might have downed the flights, and taking his own flight to a college football game while every other passenger in the U.S. was stranded.
Mullin was specifically pulled from TV appearances on the issue by newly minted White House chief of staff Stephen Miller. The Post also reported that Mullin’s accent and demeanor suggested to those around him that he was not as learned on the issues as he needed to be, and that his closest staffers weren’t, either.
The critics aren’t just in the White House. Conservative commentator Megyn Kelly said on her radio show last week that Mullin seemed “like an idiot.”
“I have zero faith in this man. Zero. Less than zero. And this is a very serious agency, one in which I would like to have some faith in the person heading it up,” Kelly said. Newsmax’s Greg Kelly also referred to Mullin and Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy as “two bumbling, mumbling, stumbling people.”
Mark Krikorian, executive director at the conservative Center for Immigration Studies, doesn’t think Mullin was ever cut out for the Department of Homeland Security position.
“He was nominated basically to get confirmed quickly,” Krikorian told the Post. “He was in the Senate, people liked him and he got along—so if you pick somebody with that kind of objective, you can’t be surprised if there will be problems.”
Whether it’s Kristi Noem, Lori Chavez-DeRemer, Pam Bondi, Alina Habba, or Katie Zacharia, the Trump administration seems to have a real problem picking individuals who can actually perform. And while this report suggests that Mullin’s days in the administration may be numbered, Trump is standing by him—at least publicly.
“He’s doing a good job. You know, it’s a very thankless job because if you take people out of the country, if you take 100 killers out and you have one person that shouldn’t be on the list, you just get decimated,” the president told the New York Post in an interview published Monday. “So it’s a very tough job.” 
A federal judge determined Tuesday that the Justice Department intentionally rushed charges against a pair of former FBI officials who worked on the Hunter Biden laptop case, all at the behest of Donald Trump.
The DOJ attempted to prosecute former intelligence analyst Nikki Floris, who ran the intelligence division at the Washington Field Office, and Bradley Benavides, a former deputy assistant director for counterintelligence. Both were charged with lying to Congress regarding Biden’s laptop contents. But the case barely had any legs at all and earlier this month was thrown out by U.S. Magistrate Judge Zia Faruqui, who argued at the time that the government’s case was tantamount to “weaponization.”
Faruqui further explained his rationale in a 48-page opinion Tuesday, tying the meritless accusations to Trump’s orders—and the midterm election cycle. A portion of Faruqui’s timeline of events preceding the charges pinpoints a July 16 national address delivered by the president, in which Trump specifically directed the FBI, as well as the CIA and the Justice Department, to investigate and charge people who participated in an alleged “cover-up” during the 2020 presidential election. The following day, FBI Director Kash Patel assigned the case to the Kansas City FBI office.
From Faruqui’s position, “there was no date of legal significance looming that necessitated the urgency of a complaint.”
“However, there was a date on the calendar that cannot be ignored,” Faruqui noted. “The midterm elections.”
“The motive for [the rush to charge] is clear: to appease President Trump in his campaign of retribution against the FBI and to score cheap political points to impact the midterm elections.”
Faruqui blasted the DOJ for compromising on its own values throughout the second Trump administration, arguing that the “slapdash prosecution” of Floris and Benavides is a testament to how far the department has compromised on its self-purported values of “independence and impartiality.”
He also observed that the general public has “suffered” under the administration’s loose approach to law enforcement, writing that “this case is not a one-off mistake” but rather one in a long string of “botched, politicized cases” where the government has rushed to charge, only for the prosecutions to eventually unravel and fall apart. He cited statistics indicating that, over the last 10 years, the government had dismissed just 0.5 percent of the cases charged by criminal complaint, whereas, by comparison, the government moved to dismiss a stunning 21 percent of all cases charged by criminal complaint during the eight-week period in which the National Guard occupied Washington.
“For years, the Administration has treated the weaponization of law enforcement like a horror movie villain coming for them. Now the threat is finally real,” Faruqui concluded. “Only the call is coming from inside the house—the White House. Perhaps, less time on the pool and more on reflecting.”
More tariffs are coming.
The Trump administration is ready to propose a new set of tariffs against the European Union, China, Mexico, Japan, South Korea, and 27 other EU members, according to reporting from The Wall Street Journal. They are expected to be announced by U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer in the coming days.
These tariffs are meant to be a revised version of the “Liberation Day” tariffs, holding a metaphorical import tax gun to the heads of countries the U.S. has any kind of trade deficit with in exchange for investments that likely won’t do anything to help everyday Americans. Those tariffs were also struck down by the Supreme Court.
China’s upcoming tariff rate won’t be more than 20 percent, the EU’s no more than 15 percent. There will be built-in options to lower those rates if countries move to reduce their own manufacturing industries—at the behest of President Trump. Regardless, these new tariffs are already facing a backlash.
“Donald Trump and JD Vance’s tariffs continue to screw over families, farmers, and small businesses—driving up costs and making life more expensive,” Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro wrote on X Tuesday. “This Administration’s failed economic policies are a disaster for Pennsylvanians—and they just don’t give a damn.” 
“We’re going to lose the WH in ’28 because of this. We’ve added less than 10,000 manufacturing jobs and are still about -200,000 what we were in 2023,” conservative commentator John Cardillo said. “All these new tariffs will do is drive prices higher and hurt American companies forced to pay them upfront, as there are no US made alternatives for much of what they’re importing. Trump is crushing American businesses and consumers.”
“Three weeks ago voters told Trump prices were the problem. Now he’s getting ready to bring the tariffs back. He clearly didn’t get the message,” former GOP Congressman Adam Kinzinger wrote Tuesday. “The rates would roughly match last year’s. Countries that cut deals with Trump would pay 15 percent or less, and everyone else would pay more. I’m not against trade enforcement. But we already tried this, and it led to higher prices for all of us. The importers got refunds and we got stuck with the costs.” 
Tariffs are one of the primary reasons voters gave for abandoning Trump and the GOP in the midterm elections, as price hikes on daily goods like meat, appliances, and automobiles negatively impacted millions of Americans. His return to them is questionable, to say the least. "

It's the biggest scandal in tech in years, and yet it seems that ultimately nothing will come of it. How do you feel about the Jensen Huang situation and how it pertains to NVIDIA's future?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Jensen Huang situation at NVIDIA is still rocking headlines, with NVIDIA's share price down today on news that the Democrat-led U.S. Congress might step in with its own investigations.
 



NVIDIA is one of the biggest companies in the world by market cap, entirely around its chip designs. Previously primarily used for gaming, its chips are now used to power data centers of all shapes and sizes, primarily for the AI infrastructure buildout as of late.
Microsoft is a key investor in NVIDIA tech for Azure, although it has been trying to move towards building its own chips to reduce costs.
NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang resigned following reports of extramarital affairs. The scandal revolves primarily around Alice Huang (no relation), who is thought to have ties to Chinese intelligence, and may have leveraged her relationship with Jensen Huang to help circumvent U.S. chip controls for exports to China.
It's been the biggest recent scandal in tech, but if you're a bit behind, here's a timeline of what has been going down.
 



I joked a bit about the situation on X last week and was taken aback by how strongly some people feel about this overall situation.
The ""culture war"" seems to have adopted the Jensen Huang scandal too, with some taking the position that affairs should be a private, ahem, affair, and not have any impact on one's career or work. Others take a different view, given Huang's privileged position, suggesting that it showcases a lack of judgment for a chap with such huge responsibilities.
There's, of course, the moral issue: Huang has his wife of several decades and kids, and his private life being so publicly exposed like this is going to weigh on them all heavily (although, being multi-billionaires probably helps to some degree).
Then there's also, and perhaps most crucially, the legal angle: did Huang's affair lead him towards violating U.S. export controls, helping China keep pace in the A.I. race, potentially harming U.S. national security?
I'm in two minds about it. I would never cheat on my partner under any circumstances, and I can't put myself in the shoes of someone who would consider doing such a thing. I have made plenty of mistakes, though, personally and professionally. Perhaps not on that level, but I'm not sure someone having an affair would cost them their job and position in other roles ... but Jensen Huang's position as CEO of NVIDIA is hardly just ""another role.""
If his judgment was impaired by his affairs, potentially even making him a blackmail target, that's a pretty big problem as pertains to U.S. national security given how close Jensen Huang has been to the U.S. political apparatus.
The cat is well and truly out of the bag with A.I. in general. It's hardly as if embargoes on NVIDIA chips are going to significantly hinder China's AI build-out regardless, as all it will do is incentivize homegrown solutions. I'm hardly an expert in this field, though, so perhaps you can educate me in the comments.
So, we're curious what you think, humble Windows Central readers. Drop in the comments, let us know your thoughts, and help me understand your position!
