52,2026-11-25,"As food inflation rises, vegetables are becoming an increasingly expensive part of the household budget
India's inflation is rising again. And the biggest worry is what's happening to food prices.
The latest government data, external shows retail inflation climbed to 6.09% in October, up from 5.54% in September.
But look more closely at food inflation. It has surged to 8.94%.
The squeeze was sharper in rural India, where food inflation was 9.09%, compared with 8.68% in urban areas.
In other words, food prices are rising considerably faster than the overall rate of inflation.
Food inflation this year has been driven by a combination of weather disruptions, supply shortages and rising input costs, say economists. 
With nearly 60% of its farmland dependent on rainfall, external, India remains acutely vulnerable to the vagaries of the monsoon. This year, the southwest monsoon was 13% below normal and unevenly distributed, while intensifying El NiÃ±o conditions have heightened risks to farm production.
Onion prices have surged, adding to the pressure on household food budgets
A survey by polling agency LocalCircles found that six in 10 consumers say household essentials cost them over 20% more each month than a year ago, prompting calls for government intervention. 
Rising prices are also forcing cutbacks: 74% are curbing discretionary spending on eating out, shopping and entertainment, while half are buying smaller quantities of essentials.
Food accounts for a smaller share of Indian household spending than it once did, but remains the biggest driver of inflation, with significant economic, social and political consequences. 
In India's latest consumer price index, food and beverages account for 36.75% of the basket of goods and services used to measure inflation. 
That is down sharply from 45.86% under the previous series, reflecting changing consumption patterns as Indians spend more on services, transport, healthcare, education and other non-food items.
Yet a sharp increase in food prices can move the overall inflation rate significantly, while disproportionately affecting people whose incomes leave little room for adjustment.
""Adjustability for a poor country like India is significantly lower,"" says Rajeswari Sengupta, an associate professor of economics at Mumbai-based Indira Gandhi Institute of Development Research, pointing to the fact that a substantial proportion of household income is still spent on food. 
Rising food prices hurt the poor the most 
The numbers bear this out. 
According to calculations by Dharmakirti Joshi, chief economist at analytics firm Crisil, the bottom 20% of rural households faced inflation of 6.9% in October, against 6.5% for the richest 20%. 
In urban India, the corresponding rates were 6.1% and 5.3%.
The pressure is visible even in the cost of a simple meal. 
Crisil's Roti Rice Rate analysis, external found that the cost of a home-cooked vegetarian thali - a meal platter - rose 12% year-on-year in October, driven by higher prices of onions, tomatoes, vegetables, edible oil, rice and cooking gas. A non-vegetarian thali became 9% more expensive.
Rice prices were up about 10% year-on-year, pulses 8%, edible oil 13% and LPG 10%, according to Pushan Sharma, director of intelligence at Crisil. ""It's no longer a few commodities that are driving this increase, it's becoming a little more broad-based,"" he says. 
When onion prices spike, households can cut back on their consumption to some extent. But substituting rice, pulses and cooking oil is considerably harder.
And food inflation is not just about what farmers produce. It is also about how efficiently that food is stored, processed and moved to the market. Weaknesses at each stage can turn seasonal shortages into sharp price spikes - and gluts into waste.
Onionomics: Peeling away the layers of India's food economy
Onions, for instance, are particularly susceptible to seasonal price swings. 
The rabi crop - winter crops, sown after the monsoon and harvested in spring - which accounts for roughly two-thirds of onion production, is harvested in March and April and stored for several months. As stocks dwindle, prices can rise sharply before the next crop arrives. 
Tomatoes, meanwhile, tend to see price spikes during the summer months, when arrivals decline in some markets or heat stress affects production.
Sharma says perishables are often responsible for the sudden spikes in food inflation, while prices of staples such as rice and wheat tend to rise more gradually but can stay higher for longer. 
""The perishables are what caused the volatility. The non-perishables, like rice and wheat, are the ones that structurally increase the cost,"" he says.
Economists say India still lacks adequate infrastructure to sort, grade, store and process much of its perishable produce.
Tomatoes, for example, can become so abundant during a glut that farmers struggle to sell them at remunerative prices, only for consumers to face sharply higher prices months later. 
With nearly 60% of its farmland dependent on rainfall, India remains vulnerable to erratic monsoons
Better storage and processing could help turn some of that surplus into products such as puree, smoothing the extremes between abundance and scarcity, according to Sharma
There are other pressures. 
Energy shocks feed into the cost of transport, irrigation and agricultural inputs. Fertiliser prices can rise. Global edible-oil markets matter because India relies heavily on imports.
To be true, India, however, has some protection against the worst effects of food inflation.
Government stocks of cereals are comfortably above prescribed minimum levels, and the free-food programme covering nearly 800 million people offers a cushion against rising prices. Rice stocks, external are particularly ample. 
There is a wider economic risk, says Sengupta. 
""Food inflation may begin with a supply shock, but its effects can spread. Workers who find that their wages no longer cover basic living costs may seek higher pay. Businesses facing higher labour costs may raise prices. What begins as a rise in the price of food can gradually become a more general inflationary pressure - the familiar wage-price spiral that central banks try to prevent,"" she says.
For India, this creates a particularly difficult balancing act.
Agriculture is a relatively low-productivity sector, and the government has long sought to protect farmers by setting minimum support prices (MSPs) for certain crops - a price floor at which it promises to buy produce, helping farmers guard against a market crash.
India has ample buffer food stocks 
At the same time, food prices are politically sensitive. Sharp increases in the price of staples such as onions and tomatoes can quickly anger consumers, prompting governments to step in with measures such as restricting exports, limiting how much traders can stockpile, or otherwise trying to bring prices down.
The broader story of India's changing food consumption makes the current inflation more interesting. 
Households are eating differently from a decade ago. An analysis, external of the 2011-12 and 2023-24 consumption surveys shows that the share of cereals in food spending has fallen sharply, particularly among the poorest households, reflecting the impact of subsidised food programmes. 
Meanwhile, consumption of nutrient-rich foods such as dairy, fruit and meat has increased across income groups, with the biggest gains among the poorest, the study revealed.
India's food basket is becoming more diverse. But diversity is meaningful only if ordinary Indians can afford it. Ultimately, food inflation is about what makes it to the plate - and what has to be left out.

55,2026-11-25,"Moneymakers and stars never face their comeuppance in Donald Trump’s world, because, as the president explained in a 2005 hot-mic moment, “when you’re a star … you can do anything.”
The president has offered to pardon newly returned Nvidia chief Jensen Huang—even though he previously insisted Huang had done nothing wrong to prompt his initial ouster from the company.
Trump told the New York Post on Tuesday that a pardon is “always an option.”
Huang resumed his roles as Nvidia’s president, chief executive officer, and member of the board on Monday. The reappointment came mere days after Huang had been tossed out of the company that he co-founded over revelations of his numerous extramarital affairs, the most dubious of which involved a Chinese businesswoman whom U.S. intelligence agencies identified as a spy and a state asset. The paramour, former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang (no relation of Jensen’s), had reportedly been in contact with an officer from China’s Ministry of State Security throughout her affair with the Nvidia chief.
Huang is scheduled to appear before the House Foreign Affairs Committee on Tuesday and then the Senate Banking Committee two days later to discuss the matter. In his interview with the Post, Trump said he didn’t believe Huang would need to invoke the Fifth Amendment to avoid incriminating himself in the upcoming congressional hearings.
“I don’t think he needs to take the Fifth. I think it’s an interesting story for people,” Trump told the Post. “I think I want him to just tell the truth, and I mean, his lawyers are going to have to advise him, but I’ve always found him to be 100 percent aboveboard, and you know, a very smart person.”
Earlier this week, the president revealed that he personally vouched to Nvidia’s board that Huang was not a national security threat. 
Nvidia shares spiraled in the weeks after Huang was ousted from the company, rattling the U.S. economy and Trump’s personal stock portfolio with it: The president’s 2026 financial disclosures revealed that he purchased between $1 million and $5 million of Nvidia stock earlier this year. As of September 30, Trump was believed to have 370,000 shares in Nvidia, according to a review by the Financial Times. Huang’s unexpected reappointment sent the company’s stock skyrocketing, jumping by 31.5 percent and adding some $950 billion to the company’s overall value. Trump, for his part, made up to $15 million, the Financial Times reported.
Markwayne Mullin is losing the White House.
New reporting from The Washington Post contends that President Trump’s second homeland security secretary is seen as a “liability” by high-ranking officials in the Trump administration, particularly after his awkward, non-reassuring handling of the Federal Aviation Administration’s nationwide grounding of flights earlier this month—baselessly suggesting that AI might have downed the flights, and taking his own flight to a college football game while every other passenger in the U.S. was stranded.
Mullin was specifically pulled from TV appearances on the issue by newly minted White House chief of staff Stephen Miller. The Post also reported that Mullin’s accent and demeanor suggested to those around him that he was not as learned on the issues as he needed to be, and that his closest staffers weren’t, either.
The critics aren’t just in the White House. Conservative commentator Megyn Kelly said on her radio show last week that Mullin seemed “like an idiot.”
“I have zero faith in this man. Zero. Less than zero. And this is a very serious agency, one in which I would like to have some faith in the person heading it up,” Kelly said. Newsmax’s Greg Kelly also referred to Mullin and Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy as “two bumbling, mumbling, stumbling people.”
Mark Krikorian, executive director at the conservative Center for Immigration Studies, doesn’t think Mullin was ever cut out for the Department of Homeland Security position.
“He was nominated basically to get confirmed quickly,” Krikorian told the Post. “He was in the Senate, people liked him and he got along—so if you pick somebody with that kind of objective, you can’t be surprised if there will be problems.”
Whether it’s Kristi Noem, Lori Chavez-DeRemer, Pam Bondi, Alina Habba, or Katie Zacharia, the Trump administration seems to have a real problem picking individuals who can actually perform. And while this report suggests that Mullin’s days in the administration may be numbered, Trump is standing by him—at least publicly.
“He’s doing a good job. You know, it’s a very thankless job because if you take people out of the country, if you take 100 killers out and you have one person that shouldn’t be on the list, you just get decimated,” the president told the New York Post in an interview published Monday. “So it’s a very tough job.” 
A federal judge determined Tuesday that the Justice Department intentionally rushed charges against a pair of former FBI officials who worked on the Hunter Biden laptop case, all at the behest of Donald Trump.
The DOJ attempted to prosecute former intelligence analyst Nikki Floris, who ran the intelligence division at the Washington Field Office, and Bradley Benavides, a former deputy assistant director for counterintelligence. Both were charged with lying to Congress regarding Biden’s laptop contents. But the case barely had any legs at all and earlier this month was thrown out by U.S. Magistrate Judge Zia Faruqui, who argued at the time that the government’s case was tantamount to “weaponization.”
Faruqui further explained his rationale in a 48-page opinion Tuesday, tying the meritless accusations to Trump’s orders—and the midterm election cycle. A portion of Faruqui’s timeline of events preceding the charges pinpoints a July 16 national address delivered by the president, in which Trump specifically directed the FBI, as well as the CIA and the Justice Department, to investigate and charge people who participated in an alleged “cover-up” during the 2020 presidential election. The following day, FBI Director Kash Patel assigned the case to the Kansas City FBI office.
From Faruqui’s position, “there was no date of legal significance looming that necessitated the urgency of a complaint.”
“However, there was a date on the calendar that cannot be ignored,” Faruqui noted. “The midterm elections.”
“The motive for [the rush to charge] is clear: to appease President Trump in his campaign of retribution against the FBI and to score cheap political points to impact the midterm elections.”
Faruqui blasted the DOJ for compromising on its own values throughout the second Trump administration, arguing that the “slapdash prosecution” of Floris and Benavides is a testament to how far the department has compromised on its self-purported values of “independence and impartiality.”
He also observed that the general public has “suffered” under the administration’s loose approach to law enforcement, writing that “this case is not a one-off mistake” but rather one in a long string of “botched, politicized cases” where the government has rushed to charge, only for the prosecutions to eventually unravel and fall apart. He cited statistics indicating that, over the last 10 years, the government had dismissed just 0.5 percent of the cases charged by criminal complaint, whereas, by comparison, the government moved to dismiss a stunning 21 percent of all cases charged by criminal complaint during the eight-week period in which the National Guard occupied Washington.
“For years, the Administration has treated the weaponization of law enforcement like a horror movie villain coming for them. Now the threat is finally real,” Faruqui concluded. “Only the call is coming from inside the house—the White House. Perhaps, less time on the pool and more on reflecting.”
More tariffs are coming.
The Trump administration is ready to propose a new set of tariffs against the European Union, China, Mexico, Japan, South Korea, and 27 other EU members, according to reporting from The Wall Street Journal. They are expected to be announced by U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer in the coming days.
These tariffs are meant to be a revised version of the “Liberation Day” tariffs, holding a metaphorical import tax gun to the heads of countries the U.S. has any kind of trade deficit with in exchange for investments that likely won’t do anything to help everyday Americans. Those tariffs were also struck down by the Supreme Court.
China’s upcoming tariff rate won’t be more than 20 percent, the EU’s no more than 15 percent. There will be built-in options to lower those rates if countries move to reduce their own manufacturing industries—at the behest of President Trump. Regardless, these new tariffs are already facing a backlash.
“Donald Trump and JD Vance’s tariffs continue to screw over families, farmers, and small businesses—driving up costs and making life more expensive,” Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro wrote on X Tuesday. “This Administration’s failed economic policies are a disaster for Pennsylvanians—and they just don’t give a damn.” 
“We’re going to lose the WH in ’28 because of this. We’ve added less than 10,000 manufacturing jobs and are still about -200,000 what we were in 2023,” conservative commentator John Cardillo said. “All these new tariffs will do is drive prices higher and hurt American companies forced to pay them upfront, as there are no US made alternatives for much of what they’re importing. Trump is crushing American businesses and consumers.”
“Three weeks ago voters told Trump prices were the problem. Now he’s getting ready to bring the tariffs back. He clearly didn’t get the message,” former GOP Congressman Adam Kinzinger wrote Tuesday. “The rates would roughly match last year’s. Countries that cut deals with Trump would pay 15 percent or less, and everyone else would pay more. I’m not against trade enforcement. But we already tried this, and it led to higher prices for all of us. The importers got refunds and we got stuck with the costs.” 
Tariffs are one of the primary reasons voters gave for abandoning Trump and the GOP in the midterm elections, as price hikes on daily goods like meat, appliances, and automobiles negatively impacted millions of Americans. His return to them is questionable, to say the least. 
The presidential turkey pardon is a silly, annual Thanksgiving tradition that spares the lives of a pair of turkeys and, more importantly, sets the celebratory tone ahead of one of America’s biggest national holidays. Donald Trump’s 2026 variation of the typically delightful ceremony was anything but.
Instead, the president used the opportunity at the lectern on the White House South Lawn Tuesday to address the American public for the umpteenth time about his myriad construction projects, including his half-finished ballroom, and supposed midterm election fraud.
“I’m pleased to report that the price of gasoline also has dropped 20 percent in the last two weeks. You notice it dropped right after the election? I wonder if someone had that in mind. A little strange,” Trump said.
Gas has dropped nearly 90 cents over the past two months, a welcome reprieve following months of rising prices due to the Iran war. The underlying reason has several contributing factors, ranging from increased oil flow out of the Middle East (including through the Strait of Hormuz), Trump’s diminished warmongering rhetoric, and anticipated seasonal price adjustments on the cusp of winter.
But the president apparently has not interpreted the deflated costs as an achievement of his administration, but rather a post-midterm conspiracy cooked up by his perceived political enemies.
“That happened with the Covid vaccine, right? Right after the election they announced we had a Covid vaccine,” Trump mused—referring to the results of the rapid vaccine development program during his first administration, which hit the market in December 2020—before immediately unraveling his own point. “But I actually announced it a few days before so I can’t blame them too much for that.”

For nearly 20 minutes, Trump prattled to the American public about his various mental fixations before eventually ceding the floor to this year’s two birds: Freedom and Feathers (whose names Trump promptly insulted)."
56,2026-11-25,"SpaceX’s Starshield lapses have created a “vicious cycle” in Ukraine, according to that country’s top diplomat.
Denys Sienik took to X Wednesday to explain how the company’s seemingly inexplicable regional internet failures and Starshield terminal restrictions have only caused Ukraine more wounds in its ongoing war against the Russian invasion.
“The Starshield restrictions in Ukraine have created a vicious circle: Neither of the key stakeholders has been able to clarify who made the decision to impose these limitations,” Sienik wrote.
“To be frank and straightforward: Ukrainians already face enough life-threatening challenges as a result of Russia’s ongoing aggression,” he continued. “We cannot afford to accept the disruption of a critical service such as Starshield as just another episode and simply move on. This issue must remain on the agenda until it is fully resolved.”
“This technology has saved countless lives in Ukraine by enabling us to effectively intercept Russian drones,” he continued. “However, the sudden restrictions, imposed without any prior warning more than a week ago, have placed Ukrainian civilians in additional danger. Every day these limitations remain in place creates real risks and potentially devastating consequences for people on the ground.”
Ukraine has suffered from several intelligence gaps since last month, when Elon Musk’s satellite-supplied internet service suddenly dropped its connection over the country as Russia barraged Kyiv with missiles. Musk initially blamed the lapse on the decisions of the U.S. military and Donald Trump, but then Trump also refused to take responsibility. 
Musk eventually confessed to slowing down the region’s Starlink connection. He claimed in an X post on October 24 that Ukraine had violated Starlink’s terms of service by using the civilian variation of SpaceX’s internet connection to attack Russia, even though Ukraine’s defenses were attempting to ward off Russian missiles at the time. 
But Starshield, the government variant that Musk has claimed is singularly controlled by the U.S., has been similarly turbulent for Ukrainian forces. On November 12, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskiy said on X that Ukraine’s front lines had experienced problems with Starshield’s services for several days.
In an interview with Bloomberg on Tuesday, Zelenskiy said that use of Starshield’s military terminals had been restricted to areas of Ukraine currently occupied by Russia, making the technology effectively useless. In the same interview, Zelenskiy revealed that he planned to appeal his war efforts to the incoming Democratic majority in Congress in the new year.
A major misstep by the Justice Department has forced the nation’s top law enforcement agency to grovel before a federal judge.
An errata court filing submitted to the D.C. District Court Tuesday by U.S. Attorney Jeanine Pirro proved to be a major embarrassment for the DOJ, as the agency was forced to explicitly apologize for giving what a lawyer for the House of Representatives described as a “categorically false” assessment of his comments about the predawn raid on Representative Ilhan Omar’s home.
The issue began last month—before Homeland Security agents raided the Minnesota Democrat’s Washington home on October 12 in an attempt to uncover evidence that her age did not match her original citizenship paperwork.
Search warrant details publicized Monday by the chief judge of the D.C. District Court, James E. Boasberg, revealed a grave error. At the time, the DOJ told the Magistrate Judge Matthew Sharbaugh—who eventually approved the warrant—that House general counsel Matthew Berry had effectively signed off on the raid by agreeing with the administration’s view of the Constitution’s speech or debate clause, insofar that lawmakers’ protections “do not extend to a member’s private residence.” 
But that was “categorically false,” according to Berry. On Tuesday, Berry said that he never intended to be involved in the case, since the House general counsel’s office does not serve as criminal defense counsel. He also referred to an October 11 email he sent the department, indicating that he had informed the DOJ of his position ahead of the raid on Omar’s house.
Hours later, Pirro noted in her errata that the detail “requires correction,” claiming that DOJ prosecutors had misinterpreted Berry’s phrasing.
“The United States’ counsel misunderstood that to be drawing a distinction based on Speech or Debate Clause concerns, but it was actually premised only on House equities regarding the place of a search, and the House General Counsel took no position on the scope of Speech or Debate Clause protections,” Pirro’s filing stated.
The U.S. Army posted a hype video for the 10,000 pounds of toxic lead azide it blew up in Iowa on Wednesday.
“U.S. Army personnel are on site in Middletown, Iowa, and have worked around the clock to mitigate the impacts of the planned controlled detonation later this morning,” the post’s caption reads. “This includes a water wall filled with more than 100,000 gallons of water, earth berms, sand filled blocks, expert dispersion modeling, and temperature-regulating tarps. Our foremost priority is public health and safety, so take a look at how we’re protecting the community.”
The video features workers preparing to detonate the lead pile—which forced hundreds of people in the surrounding area to evacuate—to inspiring music.

“Pete Hegseth didn’t have time or money to warn the people of Iowa, implement proper evacuation procedures, or come up with an alternate plan that wouldn’t destroy the environment for generations to come, but he did have the time and money to make a fancy propaganda video,” one user wrote on X.

We may finally find out who paid for early access to President Donald Trump’s Truth Social posts.
On Tuesday, Southern District of New York Judge J. Paul Oetken granted limited discovery on Trump Media to The Intercept and the Freedom of the Press Foundation as part of their lawsuit against the media company, allowing them access to who subscribes to Truth API—Trump’s blatant pay-to-play scheme in which Trump Media & Technology sells special access to Trump’s market-moving online rants, allowing Wall Street traders an advantage over the general public with regard to reacting to Trump-based market fluctuations.
“Plaintiffs may seek discovery from Trump Media with respect to: (1) the operation of Truth Social API, including but not limited to the marginal speed advantage and archival access obtained by subscribers; (2) the uptake in membership of Truth Social API by other media companies; and (3) communications between Trump Media and Defendants,” Oetken’s order reads.
In August, Trump Media revealed it had 10 trading firms as subscribers, paying $60,000 to $100,000 per month. Trump and aide Natalie Harp were sued by watchdog groups American Oversight and Campaign for Accountability in September. Truth API has also been sued by the city of San Francisco.

The paramour, former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang (no relation of Jensen’s), had reportedly been in contact with an officer from China’s Ministry of State Security throughout her affair with the Nvidia chief.
Huang is scheduled to appear before the House Foreign Affairs Committee on Tuesday and then the Senate Banking Committee two days later to discuss the matter. In his interview with the Post, Trump said he didn’t believe Huang would need to invoke the Fifth Amendment to avoid incriminating himself in the upcoming congressional hearings.
“I don’t think he needs to take the Fifth. I think it’s an interesting story for people,” Trump told the Post. “I think I want him to just tell the truth, and I mean, his lawyers are going to have to advise him, but I’ve always found him to be 100 percent aboveboard, and you know, a very smart person.”
Earlier this week, the president revealed that he personally vouched to Nvidia’s board that Huang was not a national security threat. 
Nvidia shares spiraled in the weeks after Huang was ousted from the company, rattling the U.S. economy and Trump’s personal stock portfolio with it: The president’s 2026 financial disclosures revealed that he purchased between $1 million and $5 million of Nvidia stock earlier this year. As of September 30, Trump was believed to have 370,000 shares in Nvidia, according to a review by the Financial Times. Huang’s unexpected reappointment sent the company’s stock skyrocketing, jumping by 31.5 percent and adding some $950 billion to the company’s overall value. Trump, for his part, made up to $15 million, the Financial Times reported."
57,2026-11-25,"The AI promise (left) did not match the reality (right)
The organisers of a Christmas market have cancelled all bookings and promised to refund stallholders following a backlash to AI-generated promotional materials.
David Rowland, who produced the images for Kernow Christmas Market in Pool, Cornwall, said claims they did not match reality were a ""lesson learned"".
Images showed rows of stalls beneath Christmas decorations, fairy lights and festive displays for the event last weekend. But photographs taken by visitors showed a mostly empty hall with only a handful of traders, little Christmas decoration and buckets on the floor collecting water from a leaking roof.
Rowlands Attractions said it was ""gutted"" things ""didn't go as planned"".
It said: ""The criticism we have faced and the words that have been seen on social media and across the news make us look at the world slightly differently. 
""Kindness and compassion goes out of the window so easily to knock something that people don't care to understand the full story of.""
The AI-generated view of a book shop at the market
Shopper Sammie Retallick said she felt sorry for traders who had invested money and time in the event
The company added: ""As a result of this, we are going to cancel and refund all current and future bookings and Kernow Christmas Market will take no further bookings for 2026.
""Any current bookings will be fully refunded and if any traders wish to continue to trade they must book direct with Pool Market.""
Prior to the cancellation announcement, one stallholder, Ceilidh Hurrell, said she felt ""let down"" after paying Â£180 to trade at the event.
Hurrell, who runs Nails, Hair and More in Falmouth, paid for a pitch at the Christmas market, which formed part of the wider Winter Wonderland attraction with more stalls, Santa's grotto, an ice rink and fun fair.
She said she had expected a decorated venue to ""entice people in for Christmas"".
Instead, she said traders arrived to find no advertisement for the stallholders and no decorations.
""It was just an empty space. There was nothing to suggest that there's a market going on,"" Hurrell said.
Hurrell said there was ""nothing to suggest that there's a market going on""
Stallholders have been offered refunds
Sammie Retallick, who attended with her partner on Saturday expecting a festive shopping experience, said they found ""only about five stalls"", as well as no decorations, Christmas music or lights.
Retallick said she felt sorry for traders who had invested money and time in the event.
""It's heartbreaking for them,"" she said.
Another trader claimed there were no signs directing visitors to the market area.
They added: ""People turning up to the Winter Wonderland wouldn't have been aware we were even there.
""They've told us we're going to be part of this massive Winter Wonderland, 50-plus stalls, visitors of 2,500 people plus a day, then we get shoved in a dark corner of a warehouse.""
Stallholder Ceilidh Hurrell said she felt ""let down"" 
Prior to the cancellation announcement, Rowland, who organises Winter Wonderland, said he took on the promotion of the market to help the Pool Market location.
""I thought it would be nice to help them,"" he said.
He said the AI images were ""so easy to do"" and gave a ""rough idea"" of what people could expect.
""You are never going to make it look exactly like the picture,"" he said.
""It's a lesson learned.""
Pool Market has been approached for comment.
More news stories for Cornwall
Listen to the latest news for Cornwall
Kernow Christmas Market said on Facebook the event was organised and operated by Pool Market, running alongside Cornwall Winter Wonderland.
It said: ""We are a small, family run business that started many years ago here in Cornwall. 
""This year, as an add on from Cornwalls Winter Wonderland we thought we would try and do a good deed and bring a little festive magic into Pool Market and for those who live locally in Cornwall.
""The agreement was always with Pool Market to do their bit and we do ours. We advertise it, book the stalls and they do the rest- such as signage, decorating and greeting the traders and helping them with queries.
""We are gutted, just as much as you all that things didn't go as planned.""
It said Cornwall Winter Wonderland will remain open until 3 January and Rowlands' focus will remain ""always remain on providing the very best experience for those who visit"".
It added: ""Entry is free, kindness is free, and we will always be grateful for those who continue to recognise the efforts, cost and dedication that goes on behind the scenes.""

58,2026-11-25,"Israel’s tech sector and economy have strong ties to chip giant Nvidia, which invests billions of dollars.
Save
Share
When Jensen Huang returned to lead the chip giant Nvidia this week after a scandal over his extramarital affairs, US President Donald Trump was not the only head of government to welcome his second coming.
Thousands of kilometres away, Israeli caretaker Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu was celebrating too.




Huang once called Israel Nvidia’s “second home”, and Netanyahu reflected that feeling as he wrote on X: “Nvidia’s partnership with Israel’s tech sector has created enormous value for the company and for Israel’s innovation economy.”
“Continuity in Nvidia’s leadership is good news for that partnership and for the global AI industry,” Netanyahu added, referring to Huang’s reinstatement as CEO and president on Monday.
Global headlines have focused on Huang’s affairs, including with a suspected Chinese intelligence asset; his resignation’s implications for the AI industry; and the larger technology race between Washington and Beijing. His reinstatement at the company he cofounded has gone a long way to settle market jitters. Nvidia’s share price rocketed up by 30 percent on Monday.
But Huang’s return has also calmed nerves in Israel – arguably the country most impacted by Nvidia’s performance outside the United States as it hosts the US-based company’s largest overseas operations.
Here’s what we know:
California-based Nvidia, the world’s most valuable semiconductor company with a market capitalisation of about $4 trillion, holds the key to the modern artificial intelligence revolution. Huang’s company holds an extraordinary near-90 percent share of the global market for GPUs – graphics processing units, which are specialised AI processors used in AI data centres.
Nvidia’s operations in Israel are its largest in research and development outside the US. It has doubled employees there in the past three years to 6,000 people across seven research and development centres in Yokne’am, Tel Aviv, Jerusalem, Ra’anana and Beersheba. It also has plans to build a major research and development campus in Kiryat Tivon near Haifa to accommodate up to 10,000 employees.
The Bank of Israel has estimated that Nvidia’s work in the country alone will add 2.2 percentage points of growth to Israel’s gross domestic product (GDP) in 2026, bringing it to 5.6 percent. Without Nvidia, that would be 3.4 percent growth, or 40 percent lower.
Next year, Nvidia is expected to add a further 2.4 percentage points to its GDP growth.
Nvidia paid about $1.29bn in taxes to Israel in its financial year that ended in January, according to its annual report. The company is projected to account for nearly 3 percent of Israel’s total state tax revenues in 2026, according to some estimates.
 

 
Huang’s resignation on November 12 initially rattled investors on the Tel Aviv Stock Exchange (TASE) with the benchmark indices sliding after the announcement.
But by the end of the day, they had recovered. The TA-35 index finished 0.22 per cent higher at the close of the day while the broader TA-125 gained 0.07 per cent. The BlueTech Global Index, which tracks technology companies, fell 0.15 per cent.
The Haaretz newspaper reported, however, that officials at Israel’s Ministry of Finance were concerned about the potential consequences for the Israeli economy of turmoil – and particularly Huang’s exit – at Nvidia.
On Monday, Israeli President Isaac Herzog congratulated Huang on his return, noting “his deep appreciation to Huang for his continued support for Israel and for his commitment to strengthening the connection between Nvidia and Israel”.
Herzog’s office said Huang told the president that he was “moved by the message and that he intends to make the most of the second chance he has been given”.
Nvidia is helping fuel Israel’s economic machine during its genocidal war on Gaza, pogroms by Israeli settlers against Palestinians in the occupied West Bank and the occupation of southern Lebanon.
But Israel is also returning strong numbers for the company.
The root of the tech giant’s relationship with the country can be traced back to Nvidia’s acquisition of Mellanox, an Israeli company that supplies computer networking products, in April 2020 for nearly $7bn. That acquisition was central to Nvidia’s success.
Nvidia was already able to make powerful engines that process AI workloads, but a single GPU cannot handle that workload and needs to exchange information with other GPUs.
Mellanox made the networking infrastructure that allows those engines to work together at a fast pace, creating the foundational structure of a data centre.
Nvidia’s networking business, built around Mellanox, generated more than $21bn in revenues in the third quarter of 2026. That’s about 18 percent of Nvidia’s total quarterly revenue.
At the Consumer Electronics Show in Las Vegas in January, Huang spoke about Israel’s role in the development of key Nvidia chips.
“Nvidia’s BlueField-4 DPU and three networking components – the ConnectX-9 SuperNIC, NVLink 6 Switch and Spectrum-6 Ethernet Switch – all came out of Israel. So four out of six chips came out of Israel,” he said, praising the employees in Israel.
“Pretty amazing and maybe next time, six out of six chips,” Huang added.
A review by the American Friends Service Committee, a group that won the Nobel Peace Prize in 1947 partly for its work rescuing and providing relief to Jews during the Holocaust, has found that the Israeli military uses multiple Nvidia products.
Several purchases of Nvidia products were disclosed by the Israeli Ministry of Defence during Israel’s genocidal war on Gaza, a report by the group last year noted.
“They include products that are designed for AI applications, like Jetson cards and DGX,” it said, adding that since the public database only records unclassified contracts, “this likely represents only part of the full scale of the military’s usage of Nvidia products and services.”
Israel has made extensive use of AI systems in the Gaza war – including those known as The Gospel, Lavender and Where’s Daddy? – to track and target Palestinians at an unprecedented scale, the group said, adding that Nvidia “could therefore be potentially implicated in these war crimes”.
The Nvidia Jetson TX2 AI processor is also reportedly integrated into Elbit Systems’ Lanius drones, which Israel has used to kill Palestinian civilians in Gaza.

59,2026-11-25,"

      Scott Horsley
    

The U.S. economy grew at a rapid clip during the summer and early fall, due in part to the artificial intelligence boom. That boom is also fueling higher inflation and rattling the stock market.
SCOTT DETROW, HOST:  New statistics from the Commerce Department show the U.S. economy was even stronger during the summer and early fall than we thought. That boom has a potential downside, though. It may be helping to fuel inflation. The news today sparked more gyrations in the stock market, where investors were already on edge. NPR's Scott Horsley reports.SCOTT HORSLEY, BYLINE: We already knew the U.S. economy grew at a healthy clip in July, August and September, but an update this morning shows that growth was even faster than initially recorded. GDP grew at an annual rate of 4.3% in the third quarter. Kathy Bostjancic, who's chief economist at the insurance firm Nationwide, says that's the fastest growth in three years.KATHY BOSTJANCIC: The economy was really quite robust, and driving it was AI investment spending and consumer spending, and both of those were revised even higher in the third quarter.HORSLEY: But that growth may be outpacing the economic speed limit, forcing prices to go higher. A separate report this morning clocked inflation at 3.8% for the 12 months ending in October. That's a bigger annual price increase than the previous month. Chief economist Greg Daco of the accounting firm EY-Parthenon says inflation's being driven by the huge investment in artificial intelligence, the wartime spike in fuel prices and President Trump's tariffs. Daco says businesses are less likely now to absorb those costs and instead are passing them on to their customers.GREG DACO: That is feeding into this constant repricing environment where inflation is remaining higher than it historically has been.HORSLEY: Bostjancic says the Federal Reserve will likely try to put the brakes on inflation by raising its benchmark interest rate by a quarter percentage point when policymakers meet in a couple of weeks.BOSTJANCIC: For the December meeting, I think a rate hike is locked in.HORSLEY: Many investors expect an additional quarter-point rate hike in January. But Bostjancic says that one is less certain. She says it depends in part on what happens in financial markets over the next couple of months. The stock market has been especially volatile in recent weeks. Even before the soap opera surrounding NVIDIA's CEO, investors were pondering a key question about the artificial intelligence boom.BOSTJANCIC: Is there going to be a return on investment for all of this record amount of AI spending?HORSLEY: Much of the run-up in the stock market earlier this year was concentrated in AI companies. If those gains unravel, Bostjancic says, those companies may have to slow their investment in data centers and the like.BOSTJANCIC: It also means less of a wealth effect over time for consumers, so it can dampen some of the consumer spending numbers.HORSLEY: That could mean a downshift for both the twin engines of the U.S. economy - consumer spending and AI investment. Forecasters are already projecting slower GDP growth in the final months of this year. Scott Horsley, NPR News, Washington.(SOUNDBITE OF VULFMON AND JACOB JEFFRIES' ""BLUE"")

But Bostjancic says that one is less certain. She says it depends in part on what happens in financial markets over the next couple of months. The stock market has been especially volatile in recent weeks. Even before the soap opera surrounding NVIDIA's CEO, investors were pondering a key question about the artificial intelligence boom.BOSTJANCIC: Is there going to be a return on investment for all of this record amount of AI spending?HORSLEY: Much of the run-up in the stock market earlier this year was concentrated in AI companies. If those gains unravel, Bostjancic says, those companies may have to slow their investment in data centers and the like.BOSTJANCIC: It also means less of a wealth effect over time for consumers, so it can dampen some of the consumer spending numbers.HORSLEY: That could mean a downshift for both the twin engines of the U.S. economy - consumer spending and AI investment. Forecasters are already projecting slower GDP growth in the final months of this year. Scott Horsley, NPR News, Washington.(SOUNDBITE OF VULFMON AND JACOB JEFFRIES' ""BLUE"")

60,2026-11-25," by Jason Lemkin | Blog Posts
CrowdStrike is buying Zscaler in the biggest cyber deal on record. The terms are 1.135 CrowdStrike shares for each Zscaler share, about $43.9B of enterprise value, with Jay Chaudhry staying on to run Zscaler.
The bigger number for founders is how much of it the Chaudhry family owns: somewhere between a third and 40%, depending on whose count you use. That’s well over $10B, almost 20 years after founding and after years of stock grants as a public company. A typical venture-backed founder at a $40B exit owns low single digits.
Jay got there through a few decisions in Zscaler’s first five years. Most of them run against what VCs like me usually tell founders to do.

Jay and his wife Jyoti sold SecureIT for about $70 million in stock, used part of it to bootstrap several more companies, and put about $50 million into launching Zscaler in 2007. Zscaler didn’t take its first outside investment until 2012.
His reason: “If Zscaler was largely owned by VCs, they probably could have shut it down. It took us a few years to really start getting traction in the market, and VCs can write you off and move on.”
Zscaler launched into the 2008 crash, selling cloud security when AWS wasn’t quite two years old and Azure wouldn’t exist for another two. A VC-backed version would have shown up at board meetings with slow traction in the worst downturn in decades. Plenty of companies got shut down or sold in that situation.
Founder takeaway: if your market may take four or five years to arrive, pick investors who can wait that long. Most funds can’t.
The 2012 round was $38 million, led by an unnamed investor with Lightspeed participating. Zscaler was already cash-flow positive and used the money for product development and to expand sales and marketing. It had 2,500 customers protecting about 8 million users in 160 countries.
“To date, we have resisted outside investment despite inquiries from top-tier investors,” Chaudhry said at the time.
He raised to speed up go-to-market, after product-market fit. A round you don’t need gets better terms, a better price and a quieter board.
Founder takeaway: reaching cash-flow positive before your first big round makes that round optional, and optional rounds get far better terms.
Zscaler raised more than $148 million before going public, and outside investors still owned only about 16%. That kept Jay the chief decision-maker.

On a typical venture path, outside investors own 50% to 70% by IPO, and the founding team splits 10% to 20%. Zscaler’s cap table looked nothing like that.
Founder takeaway: raise for specific uses with a clear payback, like a sales expansion or a new product line, and only as much as that needs.
Cisco offered about $2 billion before the 2018 IPO. Chaudhry turned it down because he wanted his fifth company to be the one he kept. CrowdStrike is now paying roughly 20x that.
He could only say no because of the cap table. A founder whose investors own 70%, with funds in year eight and LPs asking for distributions, takes the Cisco deal. A founder with 16% outside ownership gets to decide for himself.
Founder takeaway: before each round, think through the day a $2B offer arrives. Count who on the cap table will push you to sell, and whether you’ll have the votes to say no.
After closing, existing CrowdStrike shareholders will own about 84.1% of the combined company. Zscaler holders get the rest in CRWD stock, which fell 9.6% on announcement day while the Nasdaq rose 6.5%.
The timing makes sense. Fiscal 2026 revenue was $3.35 billion, up 25%, and ARR hit $3.771 billion. But fiscal 2027 guidance calls for revenue growth of 16.6% to 17.5%. If growth is dropping from the mid-20s to the high teens, joining a bigger platform with more distribution is a reasonable trade. CrowdStrike expects revenue synergies to reach about $1 billion over the first 30 months after closing.

Founder takeaway: if you take stock, you’re making your next investment. Jay chose the company his customers already wanted him integrated with.
Few founders can put $50M of their own money into a company, and Zscaler did take VC eventually. It took it late, in small amounts, while cash-flow positive.
Four things anyone can apply:
Jay waited five years for his market, then kept about a third of a $44B company.

62,2026-11-25,"VR headsets have a reputation for being expensive, but the Meta Quest 3S has proven that a great standalone headset doesn't need to cost over $500. Right now, the 128GB model is down to $297 on Amazon, which is its lowest price in 30 days.
That's $53 off the regular $349.99, and it comes with a bundle that makes the deal even harder to pass up.nThis isn't just a price cut. The 128GB variant currently listed includes Golf+, 1,800 in-game credits, and three months of Meta Horizon+.
For a headset that normally sits around $350, dropping to under $300 with a free game and a subscription trial is the kind of value that doesn't stick around long, especially during Black Friday when demand spikes.
The Quest 3S runs on the Snapdragon XR2 Gen 2 processor, which Meta says delivers 2x the graphical processing power of the previous generation. In practice, that means faster load times and smoother frame rates in demanding titles.
The display hits 1832 x 1920 pixels per eye at a 60Hz refresh rate, which is plenty for most VR games and comfortable for watching movies in a virtual theater. You get 8GB of RAM, up 33% from the Quest 2, which keeps the system responsive when you're juggling multiple apps or running passthrough modes.
Passthrough is where the Quest 3S really separates itself from older headsets. Two dual RGB color cameras let you blend virtual objects with your physical space, so you can play games in your living room without feeling like you're wearing a brick on your face. 

The headset weighs 515 grams, which is light enough for a full session without your neck complaining. Battery life is rated at 2+ hours, which is enough for a few rounds of Golf+ or a couple of action titles before you need to recharge.
One trade-off to keep in mind: the 60Hz refresh rate is lower than what you'd find on the full Quest 3 (90Hz). If you're coming from a high-refresh PC VR setup, that difference will be noticeable in fast-paced games.
But for casual play, fitness, and the growing library of standalone titles, the Quest 3S holds up well. At $297, the Meta Quest 3S 128GB is the cheapest way to get into standalone VR without sacrificing the passthrough, color cameras, or controller haptics that made the Quest line popular.
The Golf+ bundle is a nice bonus if you've ever wanted to try virtual golf, and the three-month Horizon+ trial gives you access to a rotating catalog of apps at no extra cost. Grab it on Amazon while the price is low and the stock is in."

65,2026-11-25,"
25.11.2026

Johannes Gottwald
				
				 (Senior Editor)			

Freedom of choice holds tremendous power, but deciding whether to place a limit order or a market order can make a difference to your trading outcome. You may get the stock at your desired price, pay more than anticipated or miss the opportunity altogether.
Whether you trade through a broker terminal or a demat trading app, the order type you choose determines how your instruction is placed and matched against available orders on the stock exchange.
Table of Contents
A market order is an instruction to buy or sell a security at the best price available in the market. Market orders are generally used when it is necessary to get a trade executed quickly. A market order does not guarantee execution at the price displayed on your screen. The price and quantity available may change between the time you enter the order and the time it is executed.
Depending on the conditions of the market, using a market order may result in execution at a price that is not in line with your expectation. An order may be executed at a price less, or more, than the quoted price. Depending on the conditions of the market, an order may be partially filled or may not be filled at all.
This type of order should be used to close a position or to purchase a liquid stock with a small bid-ask spread. Market orders should be used with care as the execution price may be worse than the quoted price.
A limit order lets you specify the price at which you’ll buy or sell a security.
A buy limit order determines the highest price you’re willing to pay. An order at this price or lower will be filled. A sell limit order determines the least price you’re willing to accept. An order at this price or higher will be filled.
The primary benefit of a limit order is price control. However, orders set at prices that are too aggressive may not be filled. If the market never reaches your limit price, your order will be cancelled. Also, if the quantity of orders at the limit price is larger than the quantity available to trade, your order will remain unfilled.
Additionally, limit orders don’t guarantee execution and may lead to an exposure on the “other side” of the market. It can be beneficial to use a limit order if you want to set a price at which you’re willing to buy or sell a security and not exceed it.
Suppose you decide to buy 1,000 shares of Tesla Inc. (TSLA) immediately. The stock’s quoted prices are as follows:
Bid: $368.80
Ask: $373.70
Since you want to buy the shares immediately, you place a market order. The exchange’s order-matching system attempts to execute it against available sell orders, starting with the best available ask price of $373.70.
However, let us assume that only 100 shares are available at this price. The remaining 900 shares may then be matched with sell orders at higher prices, such as $374.00, $374.50 or more.
This difference between the expected price and the actual execution price is known as price slippage.
Your order may be filled across multiple price levels, resulting in an average purchase price higher than $373.70. The larger the order relative to the available liquidity, the greater the possibility of slippage.
The risk of slippage is generally higher when:
Now imagine you want to buy 100 shares of Apple Inc. (AAPL) at approximately $342.94. You are only willing to pay $341.50 a share. Therefore, you would place a buy limit order at $341.50.
This order gets placed in the order book and will be executed if and when a seller places a sell order at $341.50 or lower.
Continuing with the example, let’s say the share price of Apple drops from an intraday high of $346 to $340.11. If, in this example, your limit order isn’t executed before $AAPL shares start increasing in price to $345, your order will not be executed.
A limit order guarantees price protection, but not execution protection. Just because you place a limit order at a certain price, it doesn’t mean you’ll be able to execute that trade.
In this particular example, your order will not be executed if there aren’t any shares available for sale at the price you’ve specified.
The possibility of missing a trade is higher when:
A market order prioritises execution at the best available prices, but the final price is not guaranteed. A limit order gives you control over the price at which you are willing to trade, but execution is not guaranteed.
Before placing an order through a demat trading app, consider the stock’s liquidity, bid-ask spread, market volatility and the quantity you want to trade. Choosing the right order type can help you manage execution risks and make more informed trading decisions.

Jensen Huang, Nvidia's reinstated CEO, has officially started his apology tour at the Nvidia offices. The firm had its first all-hands meeting since Huang's second coming, and he reportedly had some interesting things to say both about the situation surrounding his departure and the path forward now that he's back.
For those out of the loop, Huang had quit as CEO of Nvidia a couple of weeks ago due to multiple extramarital romantic relationships. The most troublesome of those is with Alice Huang, both a suspected Chinese honeypot and an executive of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which stands accused of siphoning billions' worth of export-controlled Nvidia GPUs to China.
On the all-hands event, Huang started off contrite, saying it was ""the hardest company meeting [he's] ever done,"" and that he's been having daily therapy for at least an hour and a half. He added that Nvidia's code of conduct, mostly written by himself, is still expected to hold up. However, the New York Times remarks that the meeting ""began somber before transforming into a defiant airing of grievances.""
Perhaps illustrating that point, Jensen reportedly said clearly that he was not the recipient of preferential treatment from either Silicon Valley or the government itself. Some may take umbrage with that statement, given that the White House itself confirmed that President Trump called Nvidia's board himself, asking for Jensen's return, citing concerns with the stock market.
Huang also addressed his active-aggressive management style by reportedly denying he was an abusive boss — yet acknowledging he goes too far on occasion, plus that he needed to keep a high degree of dedication without hurting people.
One interesting tidbit from the meeting was that he's being subjected to a monthly 360º performance review — wherein management collects anonymous feedback from multiple people who work with the reviewee, not just their direct reports. This is an Nvidia first.
The company's board investigation about whether Huang was the target of foreign intelligence continues, though a cynical person may wonder the incentive that the company now has to complete or publish the results of said work.

Wired also recently published an article claiming that Huang had started to work with a Heller, a PR firm with experience in handling public scandals, about a year ago.

Prime Video's highly anticipated sci-fi show Blade Runner 2099 premiered today, with all eight episodes available to binge. The story picks up 50 years after the events of Blade Runner 2049, but the premiere opens in 2008. We're in Los Angeles, sitting in on a sales pitch.
Ridley Scott's Blade Runner came out in 1982, depicting a dystopian version of 2019. The series opens 11 years before the events of the first movie, when companies can already buy a lab-grown workforce.
'For All Mankind' is losing some steam in its old age, but another show is picking up the slack.
In the opening scene, a Tyrell Corporation rep (played by Freddie Fox) pitches the Nexus-4 to a potential customer. If this is the first model for sale, the buyer asks, what happened to the other three? The salesman tells him that Tyrell only brings its best work to market. It's easy to imagine the company spending the years between 2000 and 2008 working the kinks out of a crude prototype over four generations.
Showrunner Silka Luisa told the Los Angeles Times that the missing history of Tyrell’s early models intrigued her. Her answer gives the salesman a good reason to dodge the buyer’s question. The first replicants Tyrell made had no built-in expiration date. By 2008, the company was letting a factory owner choose how many years his workers got to live. According to the comic Blade Runner: Origins, the LAPD formed its blade runner division in 2009, just a year after that sales pitch.
That sales pitch also changes how you see Michelle Yeoh’s Olwen, the main character of the show. As Dan Selcke observed in our early review of Blade Runner 2099, she knows when she’s going to die. Ninety-one years after a salesman offered a customer that choice, we meet someone who has to live with it.

London Museum is set to open its new home in Smithfield’s restored General Market. Redeveloped by Stanton Williams, collaborating architect Asif Khan Studio, and conservation architects Julian Harrap Architects, the project saw a decade-long transformation of the Grade II-listed Victorian building into a cultural destination.

Designed originally by Sir Horace Jones and opened in 1883, the market has been closed to the public for three decades. The overhaul of the scheme saw the museum organized across three principal spaces titled Real Time, Our Time, and Past Time, which together explore London’s contemporary identity, communities, and history through thousands of objects, installations and multimedia displays.

The entrance space, Real Time, features animated screens presenting live data about the capital, alongside works including Banksy’s Piranhas sentry box. Our Time, located in the former trading floor of the Linbury Hall, functions as a social and events space, incorporating a restaurant, shop and family facilities. Exhibits include the Lord Mayor’s State Coach, the Buckingham Palace switchboard, and Syd’s Coffee Stall.

Below ground, Past Time presents the city’s history from prehistoric settlements to the present day. Among its collections are Roman artifacts, the Cheapside Hoard, suffragette memorabilia and cultural objects including Anna Pavlova’s Dying Swan costume and a guitar owned by The Clash’s Paul Simonon.

The restoration has retained significant original architectural features, including historic shopfronts, decorative ironwork and the building’s Phoenix columns. The project aims to achieve a BREEAM Outstanding sustainability rating.

73,2026-11-25,"Watch CBS News

November 25, 2026 / 2:45 PM EST
            / CBS News
          
The U.S. economy is racing ahead, with new data showing that the country's gross domestic product rose at a blistering 4.3% pace in the third quarter. So why are investors hitting the panic button, causing stocks to swing wildly and bruising your 401(k) plan?
The answer boils down to the relationship among inflation, interest rates and the Federal Reserve, along with mounting concerns on Wall Street that the artificial intelligence boom could turn to bust.
Stocks on Wednesday show just how brittle investor sentiment is at the moment. Leading indexes plunged more than 1,000 points in morning activity, only to claw back much of those losses in afternoon trading. Where stocks go next, nobody knows.
The S&P 500 index is now about 8% lower than its most recent high on Oct. 6, putting it close to a correction, or a decline of more than 10% from its recent peak. 
Also on Wednesday, the Bureau of Economic Analysis sharply raised its estimate for third-quarter growth from its previous forecast of 3.4%. That growth is being fueled by steady consumer spending and surging business investment, with large tech firms splurging on building out data centers and other infrastructure required to power AI.
Americans benefit when the economy is growing, fueling consumer spending and hiring. But heady growth can also have a downside — especially when, as in the current moment, inflation is already running hot.
""In the simplest possible words, the economy is overheated, with demand exceeding supply.  The consequence is a blowout of the trade deficit and inflation,"" HFE chief economist Carl B. Weinberg said in a research note.
Although inflation earlier in the year was largely driven by rising energy costs, more recent signals suggest that strong economic growth, fueled by heavy AI spending, is now the main factor driving up prices. Federal Reserve Bank of Chicago President Austan Goolsbee said last week that trillions in spending on data centers are contributing to the nation's ""borderline disturbingly high"" inflation.
Newly released inflation data showed consumer prices rose 3.8% in October from a year earlier, up from 3.6% in September.
Meanwhile, the admixture of high inflation and heady economic growth virtually cements a Federal Reserve interest-rate hike at its December meeting, and possibly additional hikes in early 2027, according to economists.
The specter of rate hikes is weighing on the stock market, as higher borrowing costs could create hurdles for AI and other tech companies seeking to expand their computing capacity. And households, already stretched thin by rising inflation, could face higher costs for everything from credit cards to mortgages — a headwind for consumer spending.
""Households face renewed inflation pressures, diminished saving capacity and higher borrowing costs, while elevated interest rates are raising the cost of investment and financing across the economy,"" EY-Parthenon chief economist Gregory Daco said in an email. 
Investors are also questioning whether the huge investments by AI companies will pay off, given the massive losses recorded by many of the big AI labs. Anthropic, which last week recorded the largest initial stock offering in history, lost a startling $104 billion in the first nine months of 2026. It plans to spend nearly $1 trillion on chips and AI infrastructure through 2045.
""But the critical thing AI may never be able to do is make profits high enough to justify its declining, but still stratospheric valuations,"" Boston University economist Laurence Kotlikoff said in a Wednesday blog post. 
Some investors and economists say that investors should brace for more downside, at least in the near term. 
""As investors saw last week, pessimism can build quickly and suddenly take the wheel of market momentum,"" said Anthony Saglimbene, Ameriprise chief market strategist, in a research note. ""And fear tends to feed on itself at these readings, as falling stock prices become the evidence that justifies more selling across the market.""
Experts advise against making snap decisions during a market downturn, such as selling stocks, as it can lead to losses that are tough to recover from. For instance, investors who sold in previous downturns may have missed the sharp market gains that have often followed.
Despite investors' fears, the economy remains strong, helping shore up corporate growth, noted Doug Beath, global equity strategist at the Wells Fargo Investment Institute.
""We expect solid economic and earnings growth in 2027 to support our year-end S&P 500 target of 8,700,"" he said.

By Victor Tangermann



					Published				

					Nov 25, 2026 12:02 PM EST				

Can’t-miss innovations from the bleeding edge of science and tech
There was a strong sense of relief among investors on Monday after news emerged that president Donald Trump had ordered the reinstatement of disgraced Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang — and the company’s board had obeyed, putting the disgraced CEO back in power after disturbing revelations of multiple affairs with employees and a suspected Chinese spy.
After a bear market had gripped the stock market on Friday, his sudden rehiring was followed by a surge in investor confidence, sending Nvidia’s shares roaring back. It was an unprecedented single day rally, representing a climb of almost $1 trillion in market value.
Since Nvidia increasingly represents the backbone of an industry that Wall Street has become more than reliant on, the rally buoyed up the entire economy.
But now that a major legal and business liability is helming the world’s second most valuable company, something feels off as the dust settles. And Nvidia’s shares are reflecting it, tanking by over eight percent on Wednesday.
In fact, at around $142, the stock is now significantly below its price at the time of Huang’s ouster on November 12 and even a full day afterward. Despite this week’s rally, in other words, investors now seem even less confident than when its philandering leader was sacked.
Zooming out, jitters are filling an industry that analysts have warned could be a major bubble for years now. Tighter rules and data center moratoria could be on the horizon after lawmakers from both sides of the aisle successfully ran on anti-AI platforms during this month’s midterm elections.
Some argue Monday’s rally could be a sign that some investors needed one last push to reduce their exposure to a major risk.
“There is still a lot of nervousness out there, and some investors have taken advantage of Monday’s jump to cut their exposure to the AI trade,” Trade Nation senior market analyst David Morrison told Yahoo Finance.
Morrison also argued that depending on a single man — who admitted to an affair with a suspected Chinese spy — “reeks of desperation.”
As is to be expected at this point, Nvidia’s latest woes are dragging the broader market along with it. The S&P 500 dropped well over two percent on Wednesday, showing that the fallout extends far beyond the AI chipmaker. Analysts are also raising red flags over stubbornly high inflation and Trump’s renewed tariff war.
In short, it’s becoming increasingly clear that Huang, the alleged target of a Chinese spying operation and a married man who slept with several of his direct reports, won’t be the AI industry’s savior.
At the same time, it’s still too early to tell whether we’re watching the AI bubble pop in real time.
More on Nvidia: The Jensen Huang Scandal Is Getting Worse at the Exact Moment That Nvidia Rehired Him

75,2026-11-25,"Native Apple Silicon (ARM64) & Direct Metal static recompilation of Call of Duty: Black Ops III for macOS (zero Rosetta 2, MetalFX, live F10 settings)







Important
Ownership & Legal Notice: Zero Game Files Distributed
You must own Call of Duty: Black Ops III on Steam and have the macOS Steam version installed. This repository contains zero copyrighted Activision, Treyarch, or Aspyr binaries, shaders, or assets. ./install.sh verifies your own Steam executable by SHA-256 and compiles a native ARM64 app locally on your Mac without modifying a single file inside Steam.
Aspyr's official macOS port of Call of Duty: Black Ops III is an aging x86_64 binary built on an older D3D11-to-Metal translation layer that:
bo3mac statically recompiles Black Ops III into a 100% native Apple Silicon (arm64, proc_translated=0) macOS application and replaces Aspyr's wrapper with a custom Direct3D 11 → Metal + MetalFX Spatial renderer tuned specifically for Apple's Tile-Based Deferred Rendering (TBDR) GPUs.


Black Ops III Zombies running natively on Apple Silicon ARM64 (proc_translated=0) via bo3mac's Direct Metal backend.




For the deep-dive technical write-up, see docs/ARCHITECTURE.md.
Measured over a 10-minute sustained Shadows of Evil Round 15 stress test (Shadow Man Easter Egg, 32,880 frames, MetalFX 80% + Lossy RT + Frame Latency 2):
Help expand the benchmark matrix! If you test bo3mac on an M1, M2, M3, M4 Pro/Max, or M5 Mac (especially with uncapped FPS and 1% lows via bash tools/run_bo3.sh --perf), please open an issue or PR to share your numbers.
Because bo3mac recompiles your own copy of the game locally, it needs only Apple's free Xcode Command Line Tools (~700 MB, providing clang, make, python3, and git).
Install Call of Duty: Black Ops III normally in Steam on your Mac. You don't need to launch it through Steam first.
Open Terminal (⌘ Space → type Terminal → press Enter) and paste:
What ./install.sh automatically does:
Tip (External SSD build cache): Pass --cache-dir ""/Volumes/YourSSD/bo3mac-cache"" to keep the ~2 GB build cache off your internal SSD.
Press F10 (fn + F10 on Mac keyboards) or ⌘, (or BO3 Mac → Settings… in the menu bar) anytime during gameplay, with no restart needed:
Settings are shared between BO3 Mac.app and the in-game overlay at ~/Library/Application Support/bo3mac/settings.ini.
bo3mac compiles specifically for your Mac's detected -mcpu=apple-m* target and picks a starting graphics profile based on GPU family & display resolution (all editable live via F10):
Intel Mac user? bo3mac is ARM64-only. For Intel Macs (or if you prefer launching directly through Steam under Rosetta 2), see BO3MacFix-mawlicious.
Click Install in the macOS popup that appears (~700 MB, official Apple clang toolchain, no full Xcode needed). Once it finishes, run ./install.sh again.
Pass --game-dir ""/Volumes/YourDrive/.../Call of Duty Black Ops III"" to ./install.sh, or click Locate Game… inside BO3 Mac.app.
PlayStation (DualSense / DualShock 4), Xbox, and MFi controllers work natively via Apple's GameController framework with rumble/haptics. Connect via Bluetooth or USB before or during gameplay.
Delete /Applications/BO3 Mac.app, ~/Library/Caches/bo3mac, ~/Library/Application Support/bo3mac, and the cloned bo3mac folder. Your Steam installation of Black Ops III is never touched.

Not all tiny trailers have a story in millions of words. Some have a short tale, and the new Kleox teardrop trailer is one of them. At the time of writing, not much is known about the trailer, but from whatever little there is, we can make out that this rig has the potential to be a game changer with its interesting expandable form factor.
For those who follow my coverage on travel trailers and RVs here would know, I have something for expanding designs. This was the first thing about the new Kleox Move that caught my attention. Of course, after the Shelter Basic, Festival, and Shelter Travel models, the Move is a stand-in name, but it does have a basis, and that name justifies what the trailer does: move its position to become a shelter for two from its basic compact size. The trailer can also be used to haul luggage.
Designer: Kleox






Move for the record, features a two-piece design to fit well somewhere between a small camping trailer and a basic ground tent. It has a telescopic form factor that allows the two shells to close and expand depending on how you are using it. When on the road, the two shells can contract to become a compact ride under 10 feet (3 m), and when you are at the campsite, you can slide them apart to create a space for sleeping up to two people with convenience. We do not have information on how this transformation will happen; whether it will be electric or manual effort will be required. Either way, it will be a good contraption for solo travelers or couples that like to travel cozy.
The shells, we learn, are crafted from fiberglass. This is a good way to keep the Move lightweight for towing with a range of cars, even the smaller EVs. At the time of writing, there is no information on how much the Kleox Move would weigh, but going by the weight of its predecessors, and considering that this trailer is smaller than all of them, we are not expecting it to tip the scale beyond 500 lbs (227 kg).

For that weight, the fiberglass teardrop trailer will definitely make a strong case for itself in the saturated market. What would really play in favor of the Move is its expanding layout that can accommodate luggage on the road and transform into a bedroom for two adults as soon as you’re at the campsite. According to the information we could collect, ‘Kleox uses the space above the tow bar to create a bedroom when parked.’ Whether you like fishing, surfing or just camping in the wilderness, you can look forward to the Move being the teardrop trailer you can tow wherever life takes you on a weekend.

                Gulf travel now runs at two speeds. Pilgrimage and resort demand held through 2026, while long-haul and corporate travel fell away. Every 2027 capital and capacity plan for the region depends on which market an operator underwrites.            
Accor’s Duncan O’Rourke told Skift that UAE city hotels cut rates 15% to 20% in August and September to protect occupancy, while Gulf resort and Holy Cities hotels ran ahead of last year. Then the split moved inside Saudi Arabia. After Houthi strikes on Riyadh’s airport in October, 86% of scheduled departures were canceled and Riyadh RevPAR fell 65% to $65 for the month.
For owners, lenders and investors, the 2027 question is which of those markets to underwrite, and on what recovery date.
Developers kept building. The Middle East hotel pipeline reached a record 771 projects and 188,960 rooms in the third quarter, according to Lodging Econometrics data. Saudi Arabia holds 401 projects, up 15%, and most owners are pushing ahead, though Diriyah canceled a $191 million hotel contract in November. Transactions moved the other way. Knight Frank told Skift that pre-war asking prices no longer match buyer underwriting, and investors are rerunning feasibility studies built on pre-conflict occupancy.
The terms behind that pipeline are changing too. Gulf sovereign funds now expect returns and are phasing projects and selling assets to meet them. Owners are reopening hotel management agreements with operators over performance tests and when management fees get paid. Colliers’ Nicolas Nasra expects the toughest negotiating environment in a decade.
Forecasts disagree by most of a year. Before the October strikes, Accor expected UAE hotels back to pre-war levels by late in the first quarter of 2027. S&P Global Ratings does not expect that before the end of 2027. Since then, Accor has said regional RevPAR may fall about 12% in the fourth quarter, the region’s peak season.
Fourth-quarter results arrive after most 2027 budgets lock in. Every underwriting call made this month carries a recovery date the data cannot yet confirm.
Skift Global Forum East comes to the Grand Hyatt Abu Dhabi on December 9, 2026, with an opening reception on December 8, hosted by Experience Abu Dhabi. 
Etihad Group CEO Antonoaldo Neves, Emirates Deputy President and Chief Commercial Officer Adnan Kazim, Rotana CEO Philip Barnes, Minor Hotels’ Amir Golbarg, and leaders from Accor and Radisson will work through these questions with owners and investors placing the same bets. 
Complimentary registration is available to approved senior leaders, and standard passes are open to everyone else.
A war has turned the Gulf’s growth story into an open question. Few leaders have settled how trajectory and traveler trust hold under sustained conflict. Skift Global Forum East puts the people making that call in one room in Abu Dhabi.

                Bloom's pitch is profitable growth in a market where many chains chase room counts. Kedaara's $75 million will test whether that discipline survives as it scales up and leaves India for the first time.

Bloom, an India-based hotel brand that calls itself technology-led, raised $75 million from Kedaara Capital and plans to grow to 12,000 rooms over the next few years, up from the more than 4,700 rooms it expects to operate by the end of its financial year in March 2027. Chief product officer Tom Welbury told Skift the company has grown about 30% a year for the past five years and is now earning double-digit EBITDA margins as a percentage of revenue. The funding will go toward expanding the hotel network and building out Bloom's proprietary technology platform, with AI and dynamic pricing among the priorities. Welbury said Bloom turns down many properties that do not meet its requirements and treats profitability as a firm rule at both the unit and company level. India remains the immediate focus, with smaller cities and religious and leisure destinations flagged as growth areas, but Bloom plans to look at one or two select markets in Southeast Asia and other regions in the coming quarters. Other operators have asked to license Bloom's technology, and Welbury said the company prefers to keep the platform exclusive to its own hotels for now."
