                Dubai’s hotels are under pressure, but developers are still signing deals for branded residences. The main bet is on buyers, with brands delivering a healthy premium over the wider market.            
Developers in the UAE are still launching branded residences, betting on long-term demand as the U.S.-Iran war that began in late February weighs on Dubai’s hotel market.
Branded residences are residential properties developed in partnership with an established brand, such as a luxury hotel operator.
Avenew Development has launched The St. Regis Residences at Dubai Islands with Marriott International. Dubai South has signed UK-based Cheval Collection for Cheval South Bay. And Al Seeb Real Estate Development has started main construction on Chedi Private Residences in Dubai.
The launches come against a weakened hotel market. Dubai occupancy fell to 68.4% in October, down 16.5 points from 84.9% a year earlier, according to CoStar’s STR. Average daily rate dropped 28.1% to $181 and RevPAR fell 42% to $124. International visitors in the first 10 months of the year fell 38% to 9.7 million, according to Dubai’s Department of Economy and Tourism.

UAE developers are still launching branded residences even as Dubai's hotel market weakens under the U.S.-Iran war that began in late February. Avenew Development has launched The St. Regis Residences at Dubai Islands with Marriott, Dubai South has signed Cheval Collection for Cheval South Bay, and Al Seeb Real Estate Development has begun main construction on Chedi Private Residences. The trading backdrop is sharply worse: Dubai occupancy fell to 68.4% in October from 84.9% a year earlier, ADR dropped 28.1% to $181, RevPAR fell 42% to $124, and international visitors in the first 10 months fell 38% to 9.7 million. Ferras Hafez of Cavendish Maxwell said the branded residential sector is showing a degree of caution, with some developers reassessing timelines, and that it would be premature to suggest operators are pursuing more of these deals to offset weaker hotel performance. The clearest pressure point is rental income, since many hotel-linked residences pool units and pay owners a share of revenue tied to the same falling occupancy and rate figures. Chedi's 117 freehold homes carry no rental program at all. Most of the new supply lands in 2029 and 2030. Buyers continue to pay roughly a third more for a branded home in Dubai than for a comparable unbranded one nearby, and Savills projects the Middle East and Africa branded residences market will grow 51% year over year by the end of 2026."

83,2026-11-25,"David Fincher, the director of ""Seven,"" ""Fight Club,"" and many other classic films, very nearly had a major influence on ""Star Wars"" before Disney took control of the franchise. So, what would he have done with one of the most popular sci-fi properties on the planet? He would have focused on a pair of beloved characters, specifically the best droids in the ""Star Wars"" property.
On a recent episode of the ""Happy Sad Confused"" podcast, Fincher revealed that he had a conversation with former Lucasfilm President Kathleen Kennedy about taking th studio public before Disney bought Lucasfilm in 2012. But what would Fincher have done with ""Star Wars"" had that panned out? Perhaps not what you'd expect.
His interest lay with C-3PO and R2-D2, primarily. Not Luke Skywalker. Not Princess Leia. A protocol droid and his astromech companion. And while Fincher admitted he didn't have a full pitch, he did have a rough idea. Here's what he had to say:
""I said, 'The thing that has always interested me about — certainly from the first movie — was the relationship between [C-]3PO and R2[-D2]. That they were essentially slaves who would bear witness to the folly of man. I always thought that, them being captured by Jawas, and then sold off and being on the periphery and witnessing the Skywalker Saga, was such a crucial element to it. [...] There was a kind of moral judgment. It was fairly obvious even from 3PO [...] he had a kind of understanding as to what the Empire meant, how evil they were.""
""He was kind of trying to decide whether or not he wanted to invest in having a relationship with Luke Skywalker when he's getting his oil bath [in 'A New Hope'], and they're talking,"" Fincher added.
""Even from ['The Empire Strikes Back'], when 3PO is turned back on, and he recounts for you what he sees with the stormtroopers already being in [...] Bespin,"" David FIncher continued. ""Whenever they used the robots for that, I was always really excited. I always thought that was kind of interesting, that you have to go through the recorder, like the court reporter, to find out what it is that happened.""
Notably, C-3PO actor Anthony Daniels is the only actor to appear in all nine main ""Star Wars"" movies, while Kenny Baker portrayed R2-D2 in the original trilogy. These are characters who certainly have earned time in the spotlight, but using them as the crux of a new era for the franchise? That's a strange idea.
Be that as it may, Fincher and Kathleen Kennedy were interested in seeing if they could make something happen. Fincher, however, revealed that Disney's purchase of Lucasfilm (a deal that encompassed $4 billion in cash and stocks) nixed those plans. As the filmmaker recalled:
""I said to [Kennedy], I kind of pitched her this idea, I said, 'Let's take this thing to Wall Street and let the market decide.' And I think it was a month later or something, she said, 'Bad news. Apparently there's a $7 billion offer on the table,' and I said, 'We're not going to beat that.'""
George Lucas later opened up about selling Lucafilm to Disney, describing it as ""very, very painful."" Of course, it proved to be enormously beneficial for Disney, financially speaking. Fincher, meanwhile, has gone on to continue enjoying a very fruitful career outside the confines of a galaxy far, far away."
84,2026-11-25,"This black-on-black 1999 Mercedes-Benz G55 AMG is a Japanese-market example that was imported to the US in May 2026 following the seller’s purchase. The truck is powered by a 5.4-liter V8 linked with a five-speed automatic transmission, a dual-range transfer case, and locking front, center, and rear differentials. Equipment includes a sunroof, a front brush guard, running boards, 18″ five-spoke wheels, heated power-adjustable front seats, heated rear seats, cruise control, an aftermarket stereo, and air conditioning. This W463 G55 AMG is offered at no reserve with manufacturer’s literature, a toolkit, a clean Carfax report, and a clean Washington title in the name of the seller’s business.

The truck is finished in Obsidian Black Metallic (197), and exterior equipment includes a sunroof, a front brush guard, running boards, window deflectors, and a rear window wiper.

The 18″ five-spoke alloy wheels are mounted with Monsta Terrain Gripper tires, and a covered spare is housed on the tailgate. Stopping power is provided by slotted and ventilated front and solid rear discs. The front sway bar links were recently replaced.

The cabin houses heated power-adjustable front seats and a heated rear bench upholstered in black leather that extends to the door panels. Other interior details include trunk-mounted jump seats, cruise control, an aftermarket stereo, a remote CD changer, and air conditioning.

The four-spoke leather- and wood-rimmed steering wheel has missing trim and fronts a 280-km/h speedometer, a 7k-rpm tachometer, and auxiliary gauges for coolant temperature and fuel level. The digital odometer indicates 174k kilometers (~108k miles), approximately 350 of which were added under current ownership.

The 5.4-liter M113 V8 was factory rated at 354 horsepower and 387 lb-ft of torque.

Power is routed to all four wheels through a five-speed automatic transmission, a dual-range transfer case, and locking front, center, and rear differentials. The differential fluid lines have been replaced.
The Carfax report shows no accidents or damage reported, with a single entry in May 2026.

85,2026-11-25,"
							By 

	Mac William Bishop


	Even if one lives a long and daring life full of adventure and passion, it is unlikely one’s sexual activities will become the nexus of a geopolitical crisis, let alone the tipping point in a scandal with the potential to doom or save American democracy.

	But alas, not everyone can say this.

	One of the more unsettling features of modern life is its resonance with an earlier American epoch, a Gilded Age when the sordid lives of tycoons and power-brokers were not simply tabloid fodder, but persistent causes of concern for national security.

	On November 12, Jensen Huang, the billionaire founder of the semiconductor manufacturer Nvidia, resigned as CEO of the company he created after admissions of several extramarital affairs — including one with a Chinese national suspected of cooperating with intelligence officials in her home country.

	This affair was especially concerning to American national security officials, as the woman involved, Alice Huang (no relation to Jensen), was heavily involved with a Singapore-based tech firm called Megaspeed. That company was under investigation for transferring export-controlled technology to China, namely the kind of advanced semiconductors produced by Nvidia required for the most sophisticated artificial intelligence (AI) applications.

	










	Even more concerning is that Ms. Huang, it seems, had direct connections with China’s top intelligence agency, the Ministry of State Security, or MSS.

	Markets did not react well to the news that Nvidia might have been compromised by China. The company is a critical component of the burgeoning AI industry, the promise of which has kept stock indices growing strong since 2024 amid what was otherwise a maelstrom of macroeconomic ordeals.

	Nvidia lost more than $1 trillion dollars in market value after Huang’s departure. The tech-oriented NASDAQ fell 11 percent, and the S&P 500 dropped 9 percent, reflecting the reality — apparent to anyone examining their 401k — that exposure to AI was pervasive across sectors. Some analysts began to speculate the “AI bubble” was entering its terminal phase and panic would soon ensue, dragging markets sharply downward.

	











	
		Editor’s picks
	
	











	
	
		
					The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far		
	














	
	
		
					The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time		
	














	
	
		
					The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time		
	














	
	
		
					100 Best Movies of the 21st Century		
	








	Then President Donald Trump intervened. 

	According to the president, he convinced Nvidia’s board to reinstate Huang. Investors — those fickle creatures, as skittish as peccaries — rejoiced and plunged their money back into Wall Street and Nvidia with gusto.

	For now.

	Trump assures the public that national security concerns have been “addressed.” But he provided no details, apparently expecting Americans simply to accept that there are no further risks, even as investigations are still underway and further details continue to emerge about China’s targeting of Nvidia. 

	Although it is absurd to imagine the MSS will willingly divulge its clandestine activities, there is reason to believe that a thorough investigation may yield further details both about this specific case, and China’s wider corporate espionage and technology theft programs.

	Indeed, market reaction is just one of the many threads to pull on in the Nvidia saga, and the Trump administration’s precipitous effort to pressure a corporate board to reinstate a disgraced founder has breathed a spark of life into America’s erstwhile somnolent legislators.

	One might even begin to hope that each loose thread, when tugged, will begin to unravel the tangled skein of corruption, incompetence, and lawlessness that defines this administration.





	
		Related Content
	
	






	
	
		
					Why Do We Allow Politicians to Trade Stocks?		
	









	
	
		
					Chris Christie to Republicans: ‘I Don’t Want to Hear Your Whining’		
	









	
	
		
					Jimmy Kimmel Criticizes Trump’s ‘Fishy’ Support of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang		
	









	
	
		
					Trump Gloats About Getting Nvidia to Reinstate Disgraced CEO Jensen Huang		
	








	Here are five key questions lawmakers — and law enforcement — can ask to untangle the mess.

	First: How and why did the president insert himself into the corporate governance of a publicly traded company? Such behavior is typical of state-run firms under the worst forms of authoritarianism, whether communism on the left or fascism on the right. In either case, it is antithetical both to efficient markets and to the transparent functioning of a democratic republic for a president to force companies to accede to his personal whims.

	Second: What precisely has been the impact on national security? Further, given that Trump has hollowed out the United States’ intelligence and law enforcement communities — and the FBI in particular has cut its spyhunting capabilities — Americans ought understand whether Trump’s evisceration of U.S. counterintelligence has contributed to significant national vulnerabilities.

	Third: Who directly benefited from Huang’s reinstatement? The public must know if members of the administration, or anyone in the wider government, had advanced knowledge Trump was going to interfere with Nvidia, or acted on it in a manner that benefited them financially.

	










	Fourth: How widespread is Chinese intelligence penetration of the U.S. tech sector? Nvidia and Jensen Huang are in many ways a unique vulnerability, but they are far from the only targets. 

	Fifth: Is Congress meeting its Constitutional responsibilities when it comes to regulating high tech, especially AI? Corporations cannot be expected to strike the correct balance between profit and national security on their own, nor should they be left to operate without clear guidelines when it comes to critical tech that could benefit adversaries if it falls into the wrong hands.

	Strangely, it is the answer “No” to this final question that should offer a glimmer of hope to Americans enervated by the president’s nonstop abuses of power. That’s because this scandal opens up a new and unusual pathway for the nation’s legislators, allowing them to do something to which they have grown largely unaccustomed: to legislate — not on behalf of their party, but on behalf of the country.

	In the wake of Huang’s reinstatement, there has been a strange convergence of bipartisan outrage in Congress — particularly in the Senate.

	Even martial law-enthusiast Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., the MAGA crusader whose near-racist fixation with the Chinese Communist Party (CCP) has been the subject of meme-worthy ridicule, is apparently ready to break with Trump over his Nvidia actions.

	“I have warned the administration from the start about Communist China obtaining our most advanced chips through both smuggling and remote access,” Cotton wrote yesterday. “The controversy with Nvidia and Megaspeed is just the tip of the iceberg, and it’s not resolved because Jensen Huang has his job back.”

	The senator isn’t alone, either, with GOP colleagues in the House of Representatives providing further cover.

	“The NVIDIA [sic] board’s brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed,” a group of Republican representatives wrote in a joint statement. “We remain committed to getting answers on the mass smuggling of NVIDIA products, their diversion for Chinese military purposes, and their key role in advancing our foremost adversary’s AI capabilities.”

	The issue at stake is not merely the future of a single company. A technological edge is one of the United States’ global advantages, and the security of strategic assets must be regulated by law, not subject to the whims and undue influence of individuals, no matter how rich and powerful they are.

	










	It’s becoming clear that the Nvidia saga may present a unique opportunity for savvy politicians — one remains hopeful that such persons still exist in America — who ought to be able to make hay out of such a verdant crop of scandal. Sex! Espionage! AI! Billionaires! Corruption! Anti-capitalism! Anti-communism! Insider trading! If a lawmaker can’t craft a tune with such instruments, they probably shouldn’t be on the political stage.

	Indeed, Democrats in the incoming 120th Congress may even have an opportunity to embrace their GOP colleagues and open an investigation that is both popular and bipartisan — and theoretically, good for the country.

	One political activist has catalogued more than 117 individual actions taken under Trump 2.0 that he considers scandalous. One can debate the specifics and the scope of questionable and illegal activity by Trump and members of his administration, but nine out of ten Americans are now convinced their government is historically corrupt.

	America’s first Gilded Age was marked by extreme economic disparities, by a concentration of wealth in the hands of the few and dire living and working conditions for the masses. It was a time when industrialists and corrupt politicians worked hand-in-hand to supersede laws and rig the system in their favor, lining their pockets with ever more riches.

	Trump’s Nvidia intervention is more of the same: a lawless power-grab by an unaccountable executive who thinks he answers to no one, on behalf of a wealthy businessman in order to benefit an as-of-yet undetermined inner circle of beneficiaries.

	If members of the Republican party can be convinced to break with the beguiling demagogue who has mesmerized them into a stupor for over a decade; if Congress can be convinced to exercise its power and start reining-in America’s out-of-control executive… Who knows where it could lead? What other unholy alliances involving foreign influence, unaccountable billionaires, and power-crazed politicians might warrant a little scrutiny?

	For this reason, Americans may have cause someday to thank Jensen Huang.

 iX
The iX will ""eventually be phased out"" Down Under, with BMW arguing that it overlaps with the iX5.
   Adrian Padeanu  
 November 25, 2026
BMW is preparing to retire its flagship electric SUV in Australia, but the iX won’t disappear without a replacement. The upcoming electric X5 will take its place, bringing considerably more range, faster charging, and a familiar nameplate to the company’s EV lineup. The decision makes sense when you consider the two SUVs’ positioning. BMW Australia head of product Brendan Michel confirmed that the iX and iX5 are too similar in size and market positioning to coexist.
“The iX will eventually be phased out,” Michel told CarExpert at the Australian launch of the new X5 (G65). He added that BMW would announce the exact timeline at a later date. The iX5 will arrive in Australia around mid-2027, joining gasoline, diesel, and plug-in hybrid versions of the fifth-generation X5. That will give customers more ways than ever to buy the Spartanburg-made luxobarge.
The iX’s retirement in Australia doesn’t necessarily mean BMW is pulling the plug on the model globally. The decision announced by the Australian division concerns its local lineup, and other markets may follow different timelines. Still, the writing is on the wall for the unconventional electric SUV, especially since it’s already gone from the United States.

The iX helped BMW establish itself in the premium EV segment. Still, the next-generation X5 makes a much stronger case for electrification by combining more conventional styling with vastly superior battery technology. If you don’t consider the iX5 a direct replacement, the iX7 should fill that role when it debuts in 2027 as the first all-electric X7.
Hopefully, BMW’s engineers will find a way to retain the X7’s three-row seating despite installing what will certainly be a massive battery. We can’t imagine it being any smaller than the iX5’s 144-kWh pack, already the largest ever fitted to an electric BMW.
In the meantime, the iX isn’t going away just yet. Despite BMW’s decision to stop offering the electric SUV in the United States and eventually pull it from Australia’s lineup, production will continue through 2027. That means the iX will coexist with the new iX5 for at least another year.
There have also been reports that production at BMW’s Dingolfing plant in Germany could continue until mid-2028, although the company hasn’t confirmed that date. Either way, the iX still has some time left before Munich pulls the plug entirely. Between the iX5 and the forthcoming iX7, it’s safe to say a second-generation iX isn’t in the cards.
Source: CarExpert

Norse Atlantic Airways is fighting for its survival after warning on Wednesday that it needs fresh funding to continue operating. In a short stock exchange filing, the Norwegian low-cost, long-haul carrier described its financial situation as “critical.” 
The company said it had explored various strategic alternatives with its advisors and was open to discussions over a potential takeover. 
More immediately, Norse is in talks with selected parties to secure short-term financing. However, the airline warned that “there can be no assurances that such discussions will result in a viable solution.” 
Shares in Norse plunged to an all-time low following the announcement, down more than 70% as of 1 p.m. Oslo-time on Wednesday. The company did not disclose how much money it needs or how long it can continue operating without additional funding.

Norse Atlantic Airways told the market on Wednesday that its financial situation is ""critical"" and that it needs fresh funding to keep operating, sending its shares down more than 70% to an all-time low. The Norwegian low-cost, long-haul carrier said it is in talks with selected parties over short-term financing and is open to discussions about a takeover, but warned there is no assurance the talks will produce a viable solution. It did not disclose how much money it needs or how long it can operate without it. The warning comes despite strong commercial indicators: planes were almost 95% full in October and unit revenues on its own network rose 19% year over year, though on a much smaller schedule of 199 flights versus 355 a year earlier. Norse has been shifting its 12 Boeing 787s toward ACMI work for other carriers — most recently a deal with Pakistan International Airlines covering two 787-9s on UK-Pakistan flights — alongside a rights issue in June, a $52 million financing agreement in August, and Project Falcon, a cost program targeting $50 million in annual savings from 2027. The warning lands at the start of winter, the weakest period for European leisure-focused airlines."

        Criminal masterminds claimed to work for ‘Weihua’ or to have source code from ‘oscic’ instead of Cisco
    
A US jury has found Chinese tech giant Huawei stole tech from American companies, laundered money, and violated sanctions by building major internet infrastructure for Iran, but didn’t operate an illegal racket to get that done.
This case kicked off in 2020, when the US Department of Justice claimed entities Huawei operated in America violated federal law. The indictment led to a trial on a dozen criminal matters that has played out over the last three months in the Eastern District Court of New York.
The trial ended on Tuesday when a jury found Huawei and three subsidiary companies guilty of conspiracy to steal trade secrets, conspiracy to commit wire and bank fraud, wire fraud, bank fraud, conspiracy to defraud the United States, conspiracy to violate the International Emergency Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) and substantive violations of that law, plus money laundering conspiracy.
The Department of Justice’s celebration of the decision says Huawei was found to have stolen Cisco’s source code, after prosecutors showed evidence that “Huawei engineers left Cisco’s notes about the Cisco code, referred to Cisco using its name backwards ‘ocsic’ and described that portions of the code remained as they existed in the ‘ocsic’ code.”
Another item of evidence apparently showed a Huawei staffer photographing Fujitsu equipment while wearing a fake employee badge for a company called “Weihua” – cunningly reversing the two syllables of “Huawei.”
Another allegation the jury accepted involved Huawei staffers signing a non-disclosure agreement (NDA) ahead of a visit to T-Mobile to inspect a robot the telco created to test mobile devices.
Huawei staff ignored that NDA so thoroughly that they “sent photographs and technical details of the robot back to China” so the Chinese company could make its own robot. “When that data was not sufficient, a Huawei employee stole a part of the robot in his backpack and carried it out of T-Mobile’s controlled laboratory,” the DoJ says.
The feds say the Chinese company also “lied about its activities in Iran to banks to obtain billions of U.S. dollars and access to U.S.-dollar clearing services, which enabled Huawei to be a sophisticated participant in a global marketplace for telecommunications services — in part because of its lies.”
Huawei has issued a statement noting that the jury found it not guilty of racketeering and insisting the charges on which it was found guilty are “without legal or factual merit.”
“The five trade secret-related disputes cited by the government were all civil matters that were resolved over the past two decades,” the statement adds.
Sentencing will take place in September 2027.
The presiding judge will likely consider the fact that in 2017, US authorities fined Chinese telco equipment maker ZTE $900 million just for selling to Iran, then added an additional $1.4 billion penalty to lift a ban on doing business in the USA.
Washington has already banned Huawei from selling its telco products and personal devices in the USA. Lawmakers have little appetite to let the Chinese company return to the American market after spending billions to help American telcos replace the Huawei kit with locally made alternatives. ®

92,2026-11-25,"This story was originally published in On Background with Mark Stenberg, a free weekly newsletter that explores the key themes shaping the media industry. You can sign up for it here.
In recent months, the market for creator and social agencies has been, to put it lightly, brisk.
In September, the Madrid-based social agency Samy acquired Get Engaged, a U.S. social and influencer shop, in a deal valued in the low nine figures.
In October, Billion Dollar Boy Group bought Creator Authority, a business-to-business influencer agency, and the private equity firm Shamrock Capital, which owns ADWEEK, made an eight-figure investment in the social-first agency Saylor.
Earlier this month, the social-first agency LV8 merged with its sister company Mirage Digital on the back of a “high eight figures” investment from Velocity Capital Management. And last week, the private equity firm Falfurrias Management Partners took a majority stake in Freeman & Forrest, another B2B creator agency.
The deals are part of a record year, according to James Creech, founder of the creator M&A advisory firm Quartermast. There have been 30 agency acquisitions in the creator economy so far in 2026, up from 20 in all of 2025 and above the previous high of 28 in 2022, according to Quartermast data. 
Overall, there have been 124 M&A creator-adjacent deals this year, the busiest on record, and roughly half of the agency deals have come since July, per Quartermast. 
Behind the surge is a crop of boutiques founded during the pandemic-era influencer boom that have matured into businesses worth buying, which are run by founders who need more resources to keep up with what clients are asking for. 
A specific set of firms—social-first creative shops, podcast agencies, TikTok Shop specialists, and B2B creator plays—are responsible for the majority of this interest, while the deals themselves are increasingly financed by a new cohort of buyers, including private equity firms, strategic roll-ups, and international firms. All of it is unfolding in a frothy market for creator economy deals, which is just now beginning to consolidate in response to urgent demand across a fragmented space. 
“Creator marketing works,” Creech said. “Brands and their agency partners are going to continue to invest there.”
The current rush of creator agency activity has its origins in the pandemic, according to LV8 chief executive Griffin Haddrill.
Lockdown acted as a digital accelerant, pulling budgets toward creators at a moment when television and film had dried up and out-of-home advertising had stopped working. 
Because acquisitions typically take nine to 12 months to come together, the resulting deal volume spiked in 2021 and 2022, slowed over the following two years, and has since normalized, Creech said.
The same period produced a wave of new agencies, many of which were founded by executives who saw the emerging relevance of the creator ecosystem and launched startups to service it. Five years later, those boutiques have matured into businesses worth buying, according to Haddrill.
FOR Scoops, Sharp Analysis, and Insider Context
By submitting your email, you agree to our Terms of Use and Privacy Policy . You may opt-out anytime by clicking ‘unsubscribe’ from the newsletter or from your account.
  Subscribe 
  
For founders, selling offers the resources to keep up with clients, according to Get Engaged co-founder Ben Hiott.
“Social is becoming the center of how brands build their businesses, and clients are asking us to do more every year,” Hiott said in October.
The wave of dealmaking that followed the pandemic was primarily influencer agencies, according to Creech, while this year, the variety of targets has expanded.
In 2026, buyers have gone after social-first creative agencies, such as Get Engaged, OK Cool, and Sway Social; podcast and audio shops like Ad Results Media and Listening Party Agency; and TikTok Shop specialists including Orca, Uptickk, and Yada.
Private equity is also getting more involved in the space, investing capital after years of financing strategic acquirers, as Bridgepoint does with Samy, according to Creech. This year, Shamrock Capital took a majority stake in Saylor, Velocity became a near-equal partner in LV8, and Falfurrias bought Freeman & Forrest, among other deals.
Serial acquirers are consolidating the field as well. Influencer agency Ykone has made four acquisitions in 2026, and creator company network Residence has made two, while Samy and Publicis remain active, according to Creech. After large platform deals for Influential and Captiv8, Publicis has turned to regional acquisitions, including the French creator agency Point d’Orgue, to enter new markets, according to Creech.
For investors, the opportunity lies in stitching these shops together, according to Haddrill.
“The consumer demand was there, but the agency space was fragmented,” Haddrill said. “Now, investors are seeing this fragmentation and working to bring them together.”
B2B creator marketing has also emerged as its own category. 
Creator Authority, whose clients include Amazon, Adobe, and Mastercard, was Billion Dollar Boy Group’s first acquisition, an all-cash, multimillion-dollar deal with an earnout component, according to Business Insider. 
Freeman & Forrest, founded in 2024 by Emily Freeman and Forrest Brazeal after they ran influencer programs at Microsoft and Google Cloud, helps tech companies like Amazon and Google reach technical customers through creators.
And more of the deals cross borders: Get Engaged gave Samy a U.S. base after the agency built its business in Europe and Latin America, and dealmaking between the U.S. and the U.K. has picked up in both directions, according to Creech.
As the ecosystem matures, the next wave of dealmaking may come from the middle of the market, according to a creator management executive who asked not to be named. 
The largest independents, such as Whalar and Influential, have already sold to Accenture Song and Publicis, respectively, which leaves room for midsize independents, many of which will come to market over the next 12 months, the executive said.
Creator management firms, which run creators’ businesses rather than simply booking their brand deals, are likely the next category to consolidate, the executive added.
The buying, in other words, is far from over, according to Creech.
“It’s mature, it’s scaled, it’s professionalized. The work is not done,” Creech said. “We’re maybe entering middle innings.”
Hearst CMO Heads Out (SCOOP): The chief marketing officer at Hearst Magazines, Sherri Chambers, will be leaving the organization in a few weeks, according to sources. Chambers, who joined the digital media firm in December 2024, confirmed the news, while a Hearst Magazines spokesperson expressed their gratitude for Chambers in a statement. The departure is the latest in a series of executive shake-ups. In July, Hearst Magazines appointed Chris Berend as its first chief content and experience officer, and in October, it named A+E executive Juliana Stock as its new president, replacing Debi Chirichella, who will become chair of the group. The legacy media firm has worked hard to cultivate a reputation for calm and consistency in an otherwise turbulent industry, but it certainly looks as if the company is aiming to turn some kind of corner.
Bunny Boss Bolts: The editor in chief of Playboy, Phillip Picardi, has left the organization after less than a year, Puck reported earlier this week. According to a company spokesperson, the move was a personal decision, with Picardi returning to Los Angeles after Playboy set up headquarters in—where else—Miami. But Picardi only began the job officially in March, meaning his tenure at the publisher only lasted a brief eight months. Given all the fanfare surrounding his appointment, I have to imagine something went seriously awry. Or Picardi, who did not return my request for comment, landed an otherwise irrefusable job elsewhere. This is disappointing because I liked the direction Playboy was heading under Picardi, reporting on the state of sex, dating, and relationships in this fraught moment—but maybe the loyal fanbase did not.
BuzzFeed Taps Influential (EXCLUSIVE): On Monday, BuzzFeed named its first chief revenue officer under its new ownership, tapping Chris Poydenis, co-founder of the creator marketing firm Influential, to lead its revenue division. The appointment comes as BuzzFeed attempts an ambitious shift in direction, positioning itself as a source of free, ad-supported television, bundled with the rest of the Allen Media Group portfolio. The decision to name a creator marketing executive as the chief sales lead clearly telegraphs the manner in which BuzzFeed would like to evolve, shifting focus to its creators and video franchise talent. Will it work? The odds are long.
Sara Kehaulani Goo is president of The Washington Post’s creator network, a new division that sits outside of the newsroom and makes sponsored short-form video series with independent creators. Goo, formerly the editor in chief of Axios, launched the first show in April, and the network has since released six series in categories ranging from personal finance to career advice.
Publishers increasingly want to work with creators, but the harder question is why creators would want to work with publishers. In our conversation, Goo and I discussed how The Post’s model works, why its creator content goes through Post fact-checking, and what a media company can offer talent who could go it alone.
This interview has been edited.
Mark Stenberg: You specifically left Axios to head up this new creator division. Can you tell me about the role and how The Post is working with creators?
Sara Kehaulani Goo: I spent 12 years of my career at The Post as a reporter and editor, so it’s been nice to return in a very different role. The theory is simple: Look at where people spend their time, where they get information, where trust is growing, and where revenue is growing across media. The answer to all of those questions is creators. So, we are working with knowledge creators, people who have built big audiences around their expertise, and treating them as equal editorial and business partners.
Mark: How does a show actually work?
Sara: We mostly work with creators who’ve built audiences with short-form video in lifestyle topics like personal finance, health and wellness, and career advice. Then, we develop the shows together and publish them together. Last week, we launched Her Next Move with an executive recruiter based in New York. She posts a two-minute video to her audience of a million people as a collab with us, so it reaches both audiences. We amplify it on The Post’s social accounts and publish it on our website and app. That show is sponsored by Uber.
Mark: Why would a creator want to work with the Post? Do they share in the revenue?
Sara: I won’t get into the details, but we pay them for their time. It’s similar to a contributor model, though not exactly like freelancing, because they’re entrepreneurs. A lot of them want to focus on the content, which is what journalists want too. We help build the business around them, and we provide distribution and editorial support when they need it. 
Mark: Does their content go through Post fact-checking?
Sara: Yes, through my team. The standards are the same. There’s a lack of consistency in the creator world when it comes to disclosing sponsorships, and to quality in general, and we can bring that. Trust in creators is growing, but people still wonder whether they should trust what they’re watching on Instagram and who paid for it. We can help answer that.
Mark: It’s early, but how big is this now, and where is it headed?
Sara: This year has been a proof of concept. We’ve done six shows with six creators, across health and wellness, personal finance, travel, career advice, and entertainment. Now we’re deciding which ones to renew, and next year we want to do more always-on content. Brands are very interested, because every brand is thinking about its creator strategy, and finding the right creator takes a lot of effort. We take that headache off their shoulders. 
Mark: Creators often relish operating on their own. How do you convince them to work with you?
Sara: Having led newsrooms, I’ve always seen my job as spotting talent, developing it, and creating an environment where people can do their best work. This is the same. Creators are entrepreneurs, and the platforms give them plenty of ways to make money on their own. But being an entrepreneur isn’t for everybody, at least not at every level. Media companies have always worked with outside experts. The structure just looks different now.

U.S. stocks declined Wednesday in a volatile day of trading that was led by companies linked to the AI boom.
From the outset, markets faced a rare combination of new inflation data, a tariff announcement from the White House and lingering concerns about the strength of the AI economy.
A sharp sell-off in the morning briefly sent the Nasdaq 100 index down more than 20% from its most recent high, before sentiment improved slightly.
Major indexes ultimately closed well off their worst levels of the day. 
The Nasdaq Composite and Nasdaq 100 both ended the day lower by only 0.6% and the S&P 500 wrapped up the day lower by just 0.2%. The Dow end the trading day, slightly above the symbolic 50,000 level, up 164 points.
One beneficiary of that renewed optimism was Anthropic, the AI giant that staged a record-setting IPO last week, only to see its stock battered for three straight trading sessions. 
Anthropic’s stock traded in positive territory at several points throughout the day, but still closed just shy of its $83 IPO price.
Stocks Wednesday likely faced additional pressure due to the looming Thanksgiving holiday.
U.S. markets will be closed Thursday and open for only a half-day of trading Friday.
Investors are more likely to cut their large or risky trades before extended weekends, in order to avoid being trapped in positions while events unfold.
Even as markets pulled back from their lows, Nvidia remained among the biggest losers in key indexes. After soaring by 31% on Monday on news that its recently-departed CEO Jensen Huang would return to the C-suite, the stock ended lower by 3.9% Wednesday, its second straight down day. 
The return of Huang, a de facto ambassador for the artificial intelligence industry, briefly reassured investors that big AI firms, and the trillions of dollars they borrow and lend, would continue to grow without interruption.
Stock markets Monday staged their biggest one-day rally since April 2025, as the Nasdaq 100 skyrocketed 6.8% and the S&P 500 surged 4.7%.
President Donald Trump pushed Nvidia’s board to reinstate Huang over the weekend despite multiple extramarital affairs, including one with a woman believed to be a Chinese spy. Before the president stepped in, the board had been unequivocal that it did not plan to reinstate Huang.
Trump said Monday that he “probably” decided to intervene in Nvidia’s internal leadership decision because he was concerned about a market sell-off in the week after Huang’s departure fueled partly by investor concerns about how the CEO’s resignation would impact the broader AI industry.
But ever since Monday’s rally, markets have struggled to maintain that upward momentum.
“The risk-off tone has returned over the last 24 hours, as Monday’s rebound in tech stocks lost momentum once again,” Deutsche Bank strategists said in a note early Wednesday. 
Before Monday’s one-day rally, stocks had faced weeks of selling, which pushed the Nasdaq 100 into correction territory on Friday, which is defined as a 10% drop from its last high. It remains in correction territory as of Wednesday’s closing bell.
Shares of semiconductor stocks, as tracked by the Philadelphia semiconductor index, have plunged more than 40% since their last peak in June. Stocks of companies that make data center and computer memory products have plummeted 60% over the same period.
On top of cracks in the AI sector, “matters weren’t helped by the latest tariff headlines,” Deutsche Bank’s team wrote.
On Wednesday, the Trump administration unveiled a new wave of tariffs on the United States’ top trading partners, such as the European Union, China, Japan and a dozen more.
“It is another reminder that while the legal justification for tariffs keeps changing, US protectionism is becoming increasingly difficult to unwind,” ING senior trade economist Inga Fechner wrote in a note Wednesday. 
That drove stocks down further, with the prospect that more tariffs could cause renewed trade tensions, a renewed inflation spike and cause a flare-up with a policy that had largely started to subside in recent months.
Even before Wednesday’s formal announcement, a spokesperson for the Chinese Commerce Ministry blasted the new import duties. “Tariff wars and trade wars are in the interests of neither side,” the spokesperson said. The ministry called it “political manipulation.”
“Some investors might have been caught off guard by news that the Administration is on track to release more tariffs, but the key question about the Administration unveiling new tariffs from the Section 301 overcapacity investigation has never been an issue of whether but when,” wrote Sarah Bianchi, chief strategist of international political affairs at Evercore ISI.
Compounding the negative pressure facing investors Wednesday, the Federal Reserve’s preferred inflation gauge moved further away from its 2% goal.
The personal consumption expenditures index was 3.8% for October, up from 3.6% in September. Even excluding food and energy, the “core” inflation reading rose to 3.3%. It had been 3.1% the previous month.
The rise in core inflation readings is particularly noteworthy because it can indicate that higher gas prices sparked by the Iran war are spilling over into the rest of the economy.
It also raises the likelihood that the Fed will raise interest rates at its next meeting, which concludes with a rate decision Dec. 9. 
Last week, a top Fed policymaker said the central bank needed to “shock people” with a larger rate hike than is typical.
“I’m very, very concerned about inflation,” Fed governor Christopher Waller said in a CNBC interview. “It’s kind of headed in the wrong direction, and it’s spreading out.” 
Waller said the Fed needed to take “more aggressive actions,” such as hiking by 0.50%, more than the Fed’s usual 0.25% rate increase. “You might need to, like, shock people,” he said.
A rate hike would carry its own complications, however. As trillion-dollar tech giants borrow hundreds of billions of dollars to build out AI data centers and chips, any increase to interest rates could quickly raise their massive costs.
It could also hurt an already pained U.S. consumer. Mortgage rates continue to hover well above 7%, and a rate hike could push borrowing costs higher for auto loans, credit cards, personal loans and other forms of credit.
Steve Kopack is a senior reporter at NBC News covering business and the economy.

November 25, 2026 / 4:17 PM EST
            / CBS News
          
Prediction market companies tout themselves as a place where anyone can make a quick buck. Don't bet on it, recent data suggests.
Take the U.S. midterm elections, which generated ample business for prediction market platforms as users wagered on who would win congressional races nationwide. And a few people did, in fact, clean up — very few.  
On Polymarket, the top 1% of accounts took home 91% of the profits from midterm bets, totaling roughly $23 million, according to research this week from the Anti-Corruption Data Collective (ACDC), a nonprofit advocacy group focused on corporate and financial corruption. The bottom 99% of traders lost a total of $15 million.
Of the nearly 82,000 accounts that the group analyzed, more than half lost money on Polymarket's midterm-focused markets.
""At a time [when] affordability was a major issue at the ballot box, average traders handed over their cash to an elite few, including many professional and automated traders,"" ACDC said in its study. 
According to Polymarket, 95% of the markets on its platform accurately predicted the outcome of this year's midterm elections.
""Prediction markets have become an important source of real-time election information as they bring together the knowledge and insights of informed participants to show the public how likely different outcomes are,"" a Polymarket spokesperson said in an email to CBS News. ""Polymarket continues to be a destination for timely, accurate information that anyone can follow as events unfold, whether they trade or not.""
According to the Pew Research Center, Polymarket and rival prediction market Kalshi hosted a combined 34,000 contracts related to the midterms, up from 2,300 in 2024. The midterms generated $2.7 billion in trading volume on the platforms, compared with $8.5 billion during the 2024 presidential and congressional elections, Pew found.
Other recent data point to the challenge of winning money on prediction markets.
Findings released by the New York City Council on Tuesday show that, from Jan. 1 to Aug. 11, New York City residents who traded on Kalshi lost a total of $17.6 million. So-called institutional traders on the platform won $210 million over the same period.
On Wall Street, institutional investors generally refer to financial firms, market makers and other pros who invest money on behalf of their clients. In Kalshi's case, it also encapsulates retail traders, solo forecasters and two-person teams that use LLCs for tax or business purposes, according to Elisabeth Diana, a spokesperson for the prediction market.
""Most winning traders [are] labeled as 'institutional' even though they are often 'individuals,'"" she said in an email, adding, ""These are not Wall Street institutions.""
Diana also said that, as in other financial markets, a trader's skill level matters.
""Traders who get better at forecasting can earn more, and their trades make the market more efficient,"" she said. ""Individuals do worse day trading on the stock markets than professionals. The only difference here is that prediction markets give more people an opportunity to be a 'professional.'""

95,2026-11-25,"Anthropic’s stock closed down 6.5 percent on Tuesday before falling another 4.4 percent on Wednesday morning. The AI startup’s current share price of $77.42 is well below its $111.50 IPO high just five trading sessions after its record-breaking Nasdaq debut.
CNBC reports that shares of Anthropic, trading under the ticker ANT, opened at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, November 19. At Wednesday’s market open, they had fallen well under the IPO price, putting investors who bought shares at the debut deeply in the red. The company raised $81.3 billion in the IPO, which was heavily oversubscribed. According to prior CNBC reporting, roughly half of institutional investors who placed orders received no shares at all.
Breitbart News previously reported that shares of Anthropic fell 22 percent from its IPO high almost immediately.
The decline came as a broader selloff hit chip and AI infrastructure stocks. Nvidia, AMD and Intel all fell during intraday trading Tuesday, as did neocloud companies CoreWeave, IREN and Nebius. The moves followed a volatile stretch for Nvidia in particular. The company reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO on Monday, reversing an earlier ouster earlier this month, and its shares surged more than 30 percent that day before giving back some of those gains Tuesday.
Even as Anthropic’s stock slid, three Wall Street firms initiated coverage this week with bullish outlooks. Canaccord Genuity started coverage with a buy rating and a $135 price target in a Tuesday note, calling Anthropic “one of the most important enterprise technology franchises of the AI era” and arguing its “opportunity is considerably larger than traditional software spending.” The firm’s analysts acknowledged the uncertainty facing the company but said the upside outweighs it: “The risks are unusually large, particularly around infrastructure commitments and the durability of competitive differentiation, but we believe the combination of exceptional growth, emerging earnings capacity, and an attractive entry valuation support a compelling investment opportunity.”
Wedbush, which initiated coverage Monday with an outperform rating and a $120 price target, described the current environment as a “tumultuous market” but said Anthropic stands to “capture a uniquely large addressable market.” Looking past near-term volatility across the AI sector, Wedbush’s analysts wrote: “While there’s going to continue to be many ups and downs, and winners and losers as this ecosystem gets built, in our view there is no real question that it does get built over time. The genie is out of the bottle. And ANT is a strong play on that theme.”
Oppenheimer offered the most aggressive target of the three, initiating coverage Tuesday with an outperform rating and a $150 price target, more than 80 percent above the IPO price. The firm’s analysts called their position a “bullish stance,” pointing to rising customer adoption that gives Anthropic “a substantial runway.” They weighed that optimism against ongoing threats from rivals and the costs of building out computing capacity, writing: “While competition and compute commitments remain risks, we believe Anthropic can translate its leadership into durable growth and operating leverage.”
Breitbart News previously reported that Anthropic’s IPO will funnel at least $156 billion to Effective Altruism (EA) and leftist causes:
Anthropic’s leadership grew out of the effective altruism (EA) movement, and that shows up directly in the paperwork. The company set aside five percent of its shares, almost 850 million of them, for a Public Benefit Fund. At Anthropic’s trading price of around $95, that endowment is worth about $80 billion. Its first grant is $200 million to study how AI affects workers.
The fund can keep growing. Each time Anthropic’s valuation climbs more than 50 percent above its $83 IPO price, the fund gains another two percent of shares. The next trigger point is about $124.50 per share.
Anthropic also donated $50 million to the city of San Francisco last week, though a company spokesperson said that gift did not come from the Public Benefit Fund.
These and other recent AI developments were presaged in the breakout book by Breitbart News social media director Wynton titled: Code Red: The Left, the Right, China, and the Race to Control AI, the first and only New York Times bestselling AI book written from a conservative perspective. “AI isn’t just a tool,” says Hall. “It is also political power. The challenge is knowing how to seize the ‘roses’ of AI while averting its many ‘landmines.’”
